16	
17	# LaTeX 论文写作全流程
18	
19	ML/AI 顶会论文从草稿到投稿的完整指南。整合 Orchestra Research、VibeSkills、claim2evidence 三方最佳实践。
20	
21	---
22	
23	## 1. 工作模式
24	
25	开始前选择模式：
26	
27	| 模式 | 说明 | 适用 |
28	|------|------|------|
29	| **Build** | 从实验笔记/结果/代码生成论文 | 新稿 |
30	| **Improve** | 改写已有章节，强化论证 | 修改稿 |
31	| **Audit** | 输出风险列表 + 缺失证据 + 修复建议 | 投前检查 |
32	
33	---
34	
35	## 2. Claim-Evidence 方法论
36	
37	**核心原则：论文是一个有中心贡献的故事，不是实验的堆砌。**
38	
39	### 2.1 一句话贡献
40	
41	在写任何内容之前，必须能用一句话说清贡献。如果不能，说明还没准备好写论文。
42	
43	### 2.2 Claim-Evidence Map
44	
45	为每个 contribution 建立 claim→evidence 映射表：
46	
47	| Contribution | Claim | Evidence | Table/Figure |
48	|-------------|-------|----------|-------------|
49	| C1: 方法 X 在 Y 上 SOTA | 比所有 baseline 高 ≥2% | 5-seed 平均，p<0.05 | Table 1 |
50	| C2: 消融证明 Z 是关键 | 去掉 Z 后掉 >5% | 消融实验 | Table 2 |
51	| C3: 计算效率 | 比 baseline 快 3× | FLOPs + wall-clock | Figure 3 |
52	
53	**没有直接证据支撑的 claim 必须删除或降级为 discussion。**
54	
55	### 2.3 章节起草顺序
56	
57	```
58	1. Introduction + Experiments（先确定 claim 和 evidence）
59	2. Method（评估问题稳定后再写细节）
60	3. Related Work
61	4. Abstract + Title（核心 claim 定型后最后写）
62	```
63	
64	### 2.4 Quality Gates
65	
66	投稿前必须通过：
67	- [ ] 前两段出现显式 novelty 声明
68	- [ ] 每个 contribution 有可验证的 evidence
69	- [ ] Abstract / Intro / Conclusion 的 claim 一致
70	- [ ] Limitations 真实且技术性诚实
71	- [ ] 审稿人两分钟内能识别 delta
72	
73	---
74	
75	## 3. 引用防幻觉（CRITICAL）
76	
77	**AI 生成引用有 ~40% 错误率。绝对禁止凭记忆写 BibTeX。**
78	
79	### 3.1 黄金法则
80	
81	```
82	IF 无法程序化获取引用:
83	    → 标记 [CITATION NEEDED] 或 \cite{PLACEHOLDER_verify}
84	    → 明确告知用户
85	    → 绝不编造看似合理的引用
86	```
87	
88	### 3.2 五步验证流程
89	
90	```
91	1. SEARCH  → Semantic Scholar API 搜索关键词
92	2. VERIFY  → 至少 2 个来源确认论文存在（S2 + arXiv/CrossRef）
93	3. RETRIEVE → 通过 DOI 获取 BibTeX（程序化，非记忆）
94	4. CHECK   → 确认你引用的 claim 确实出自该论文
95	5. ADD     → 加入 .bib 文件
96	```
97	
98	### 3.3 API 速查
99	
100	| API | 用途 | 限制 |
101	|-----|------|------|
102	| **Semantic Scholar** | ML/AI 论文搜索 | 1 RPS (免费) |
103	| **CrossRef** | DOI → BibTeX | 需 mailto 头 |
104	| **arXiv** | 预印本搜索 | 3s 间隔 |
105	
106	```python
107	# DOI → BibTeX（最可靠的方式）
108	import requests
109	def doi_to_bibtex(doi: str) -> str:
110	    r = requests.get(f"https://doi.org/{doi}",
111	                     headers={"Accept": "application/x-bibtex"})
112	    r.raise_for_status()
113	    return r.text
114	
115	# Semantic Scholar 搜索
116	from semanticscholar import SemanticScholar
117	sch = SemanticScholar()
118	results = sch.search_paper("attention mechanism transformers", limit=5)
119	for p in results:
120	    print(f"{p.title} | DOI: {p.externalIds.get('DOI', 'N/A')}")
121	```
122	
123	### 3.4 引用处理规则
124	
125	| 情况 | 处理 |
126	|------|------|
127	| 找到论文 + DOI + BibTeX | 直接用 |
128	| 找到论文，无 DOI | 用 arXiv BibTeX |
129	| 论文存在但无法获取 BibTeX | 标记 placeholder，告知用户 |
130	| 不确定论文是否存在 | 标记 `[CITATION NEEDED]`，告知用户 |
131	| "我觉得有篇关于 X 的论文" | **绝不引用** — 先搜索或标记 |
132

134	
135	## 4. 顶会模板速查
136	
137	### 4.1 ML/AI 会议
138	
139	| 会议 | 页数限制 | 主文件 | 样式文件 | 必须项 |
140	|------|---------|--------|---------|--------|
141	| **NeurIPS** | 9 页 | `main.tex` | `neurips.sty` | Paper Checklist |
142	| **ICML** | 8 页 (+1 camera-ready) | `example_paper.tex` | `icml2026.sty` | Broader Impact |
143	| **ICLR** | 9 页 (+1) | `iclr_conference.tex` | `iclr_conference.sty` | LLM 使用声明 |
144	| **ACL/EMNLP** | 8 页 (long) | `acl_latex.tex` | `acl.sty` | Limitations 必须 |
145	| **AAAI** | 7 页 (+1) | `aaai_template.tex` | `aaai.sty` | 严格样式 |
146	| **COLM** | 9 页 (+1) | `colm_conference.tex` | `colm_conference.sty` | 语言模型聚焦 |
147	
148	### 4.2 系统会议
149	
150	| 会议 | 页数限制 | 模板类型 | 关键要求 |
151	|------|---------|---------|---------|
152	| **OSDI** | 12 页 (+2) | USENIX `usenix.sty` | Research / Operational 双 track |
153	| **NSDI** | 12 页 | USENIX | Introduction 预筛 |
154	| **ASPLOS** | 12 页 | ACM `acmart.cls (sigplan)` | 前 2 页快速评审 |
155	| **SOSP** | 12 页 | ACM `acmart.cls (sigplan)` | 可选 Artifact Evaluation |
156	
157	### 4.3 模板使用原则
158	
159	```bash
160	# 1. 复制整个模板目录（不要只拷 .tex）
161	cp -r templates/neurips2025/ ~/papers/my-paper/
162	
163	# 2. 先编译未修改的模板，确认能跑通
164	latexmk -pdf main.tex
165	
166	# 3. 逐章替换内容，频繁编译
167	```
168	
169	**禁止事项：**
170	- 不要修改 `.sty` 文件
171	- 不要在会议模板间复制 `\usepackage` 行
172	- 不要只复制 `.tex` 而不带 `.sty/.bst`
173	
174	---
175	
176	## 5. 写作规范
177	
178	### 5.1 时间分配（Neel Nanda）
179	
180	花**大致相等**的时间在：
181	1. Abstract
182	2. Introduction
183	3. 图表
184	4. **其余所有内容加起来**
185	
186	审稿人读论文的顺序：标题 → 摘要 → 引言 → 图表 → 也许正文。
187	
188	### 5.2 Abstract 五句法（Sebastian Farquhar）
189	
190	```
191	1. 你做了什么（"We introduce/prove/demonstrate..."）
192	2. 为什么这很难且重要
193	3. 怎么做的（含专业关键词以便检索）
194	4. 有什么证据
195	5. 最惊人的数字/结果
196	```
197	
198	删除一切泛泛的开头：~~"Large language models have achieved remarkable success..."~~
199	
200	### 5.3 句子级清晰度（Gopen & Swan）
201	
202	| 原则 | 规则 |
203	|------|------|
204	| 主谓紧邻 | 不要在主语和动词之间插入长从句 |
205	| 重点在句尾 | 把关键数字/结论放在句末 |
206	| 旧信息在前 | 先放读者已知的内容，再引入新概念 |
207	| 动词表达动作 | "We analyzed" 而非 "We performed an analysis" |
208	
209	### 5.4 常见修改
210	
211	| 问题 | 修复 |
212	|------|------|
213	| Abstract 太泛 | 删第一句（如果它能放在任何 ML 论文前面） |
214	| Introduction 超 1.5 页 | 背景挪到 Related Work |
215	| 实验没有显式 claim | 每个实验前加 "This tests whether..." |
216	| 缺统计显著性 | 加 error bars + 运行次数 + 计算方法 |
217	
218	---
219	
220	## 6. 投稿工程链
221	
222	### 6.1 构建
223	
224	```bash
225	# 推荐：latexmk 一键构建
226	latexmk -pdf -interaction=nonstopmode -halt-on-error main.tex
227	
228	# 手动：需要多次编译
229	pdflatex main.tex && bibtex main && pdflatex main.tex && pdflatex main.tex
230	```
231	
232	### 6.2 Lint + 格式化
233	
234	```bash
235	# Lint: 检查常见 LaTeX 问题
236	chktex main.tex
237	
238	# 格式化: 减少 diff 噪声
239	latexindent -w main.tex
240	```
241	
242	### 6.3 图表格式
243	
244	| 类型 | 格式 | 说明 |
245	|------|------|------|
246	| 矢量图（plot、diagram） | PDF | matplotlib: `plt.savefig('fig.pdf')` |
247	| 照片/heatmap | PNG 600dpi 或 TIFF | 栅格图 |
248	| 表格 | `booktabs` 包 | `\toprule/\midrule/\bottomrule` |
249	
250	```latex
251	\usepackage{booktabs}
252	\begin{tabular}{lcc}
253	\toprule
254	Method & Accuracy $\uparrow$ & Latency $\downarrow$ \\
255	\midrule
256	Baseline & 85.2 & 45ms \\
257	\textbf{Ours} & \textbf{92.1} & 38ms \\
258	\bottomrule
259	\end{tabular}
260	```
261	
262	**图表规范：**
263	- 用色盲友好色板（Okabe-Ito 或 Paul Tol）
264	- 图内不放标题（用 caption）
265	- Caption 独立可理解（不依赖正文）
266	- 最佳值加粗，标注方向符号（↑ 越高越好，↓ 越低越好）
267	
268	### 6.4 投稿打包
269	
270	```bash
271	# 清理辅助文件
272	latexmk -c
273	
274	# 打包投稿
275	zip -r submission.zip main.tex *.sty *.bst *.bib figures/ --exclude='*.aux' --exclude='*.log'
276	```
277	
278	### 6.5 常见失败点
279	
280	| 问题 | 解决 |
281	|------|------|
282	| 图片格式混用导致编译失败 | 统一用 PDF |
283	| 字体未嵌入 | `pdffonts output.pdf` 检查 |
284	| Overfull box 过多 | 关键位置手动调行 |
285	| 引用缺字段 | 检查 DOI/年份/期刊名 |
286	| 超页数 | 用 `\vspace{-Xpt}` 微调，或精简内容 |
287	
288	---
289	
290	## 7. 会议转投
291	
292	当论文被拒或转投其他会议时：
293	
294	### 7.1 ML/AI 会议间转换
295	
296	| 转换 | 页数变化 | 关键调整 |
297	|------|---------|---------|
298	| NeurIPS → ICML | 9→8 | 删 1 页，加 Broader Impact |
299	| ICML → ICLR | 8→9 | 可扩展实验 + LLM 声明 |
300	| NeurIPS → ACL | 9→8 | 按 NLP 惯例重组 + Limitations |
301	| ICLR → AAAI | 9→7 | 大幅精简，严格样式 |
302	
303	### 7.2 转投原则
304	
305	1. **永远用目标模板重新开始** — 复制目标模板，迁移内容，不要合并 preamble
306	2. **只迁移正文内容** — 不复制 `\usepackage`、header/footer 命令
307	3. **根据审稿意见改进** — 但不要在新稿中提及旧投稿（双盲）
308	4. **检查目标会议特殊要求** — checklist、ethics statement、LLM 声明等
309	
310	---
311	
312	## 8. 投前清单
313	
314	### 通用清单
315	
316	- [ ] Abstract/Intro/Conclusion 的 claim 一致
317	- [ ] 每个 contribution 有对应 evidence
318	- [ ] 所有引用已程序化验证（无幻觉引用）
319	- [ ] Error bars + 运行次数 + 统计方法
320	- [ ] Limitations 章节存在且诚实
321	- [ ] 匿名化（双盲审稿）
322	- [ ] 符合页数限制
323	- [ ] 所有图表有自包含 caption
324	- [ ] `.bib` 中无缺失字段
325	- [ ] PDF 编译无 warning
326	
327	### 会议特定
328	
329	| 会议 | 额外要求 |
330	|------|---------|
331	| NeurIPS | 16 项 Paper Checklist（附录，不计页数） |
332	| ICML | Broader Impact Statement |
333	| ICLR | LLM 使用声明 |
334	| ACL/EMNLP | Limitations 章节（必须），Ethics Statement |
335	| AAAI | 严格样式文件合规 |

340

343	- [Farquhar: How to Write ML Papers](https://sebastianfarquhar.com/on-research/2024/11/04/how_to_write_ml_papers/) — 五句法 Abstract
344	- [Gopen & Swan: Science of Scientific Writing](https://cseweb.ucsd.edu/~swanson/papers/science-of-writing.pdf) — 读者预期 7 原则
345	- [Lipton: Heuristics for Scientific Writing](https://www.approximatelycorrect.com/2018/01/29/heuristics-technical-scientific-writing-machine-learning-perspective/) — 用词
346	- [Perez: Easy Paper Writing Tips](https://ethanperez.net/easy-paper-writing-tips/) — 微观清晰度

      "content": "---\nname: latex-paper\ndescription: ML 顶会论文 LaTeX 写作全流程——Claim-Evidence 方法论、引用防幻觉、顶会模板速查、投稿工程链、会议转投。写论文/投稿时参考。\nscope: global\ntags:\n- latex\n- paper-writing\n- academic\n- citation\n- submission\nversion: 1\nroles:\n- agent\n- worker\n---\n\n# LaTeX 论文写作全流程\n\nML/AI 顶会论文从草稿到投稿的完整指南。整合 Orchestra Research、VibeSkills、claim2evidence 三方最佳实践。\n\n---\n\n## 1. 工作模式\n\n开始前选择模式：\n\n| 模式 | 说明 | 适用 |\n|------|------|------|\n| **Build** | 从实验笔记/结果/代码生成论文 | 新稿 |\n| **Improve** | 改写已有章节，强化论证 | 修改稿 |\n| **Audit** | 输出风险列表 + 缺失证据 + 修复建议 | 投前检查 |\n\n---\n\n## 2. Claim-Evidence 方法论\n\n**核心原则：论文是一个有中心贡献的故事，不是实验的堆砌。**\n\n### 2.1 一句话贡献\n\n在写任何内容之前，必须能用一句话说清贡献。如果不能，说明还没准备好写论文。\n\n### 2.2 Claim-Evidence Map\n\n为每个 contribution 建立 claim→evidence 映射表：\n\n| Contribution | Claim | Evidence | Table/Figure |\n|-------------|-------|----------|-------------|\n| C1: 方法 X 在 Y 上 SOTA | 比所有 baseline 高 ≥2% | 5-seed 平均，p<0.05 | Table 1 |\n| C2: 消融证明 Z 是关键 | 去掉 Z 后掉 >5% | 消融实验 | Table 2 |\n| C3: 计算效率 | 比 baseline 快 3× | FLOPs + wall-clock | Figure 3 |\n\n**没有直接证据支撑的 claim 必须删除或降级为 discussion。**\n\n### 2.3 章节起草顺序\n\n```\n1. Introduction + Experiments（先确定 claim 和 evidence）\n2. Method（评估问题稳定后再写细节）\n3. Related Work\n4. Abstract + Title（核心 claim 定型后最后写）\n```\n\n### 2.4 Quality Gates\n\n投稿前必须通过：\n- [ ] 前两段出现显式 novelty 声明\n- [ ] 每个 contribution 有可验证的 evidence\n- [ ] Abstract / Intro / Conclusion 的 claim 一致\n- [ ] Limitations 真实且技术性诚实\n- [ ] 审稿人两分钟内能识别 delta\n\n---\n\n## 3. 引用防幻觉（CRITICAL）\n\n**AI 生成引用有 ~40% 错误率。绝对禁止凭记忆写 BibTeX。**\n\n### 3.1 黄金法则\n\n```\nIF 无法程序化获取引用:\n    → 标记 [CITATION NEEDED] 或 \\cite{PLACEHOLDER_verify}\n    → 明确告知用户\n    → 绝不编造看似合理的引用\n```\n\n### 3.2 五步验证流程\n\n```\n1. SEARCH  → Semantic Scholar API 搜索关键词\n2. VERIFY  → 至少 2 个来源确认论文存在（S2 + arXiv/CrossRef）\n3. RETRIEVE → 通过 DOI 获取 BibTeX（程序化，非记忆）\n4. CHECK   → 确认你引用的 claim 确实出自该论文\n5. ADD     → 加入 .bib 文件\n```\n\n### 3.3 API 速查\n\n| API | 用途 | 限制 |\n|-----|------|------|\n| **Semantic Scholar** | ML/AI 论文搜索 | 1 RPS (免费) |\n| **CrossRef** | DOI → BibTeX | 需 mailto 头 |\n| **arXiv** | 预印本搜索 | 3s 间隔 |\n\n```python\n# DOI → BibTeX（最可靠的方式）\nimport requests\ndef doi_to_bibtex(doi: str) -> str:\n    r = requests.get(f\"https://doi.org/{doi}\",\n                     headers={\"Accept\": \"application/x-bibtex\"})\n    r.raise_for_status()\n    return r.text\n\n# Semantic Scholar 搜索\nfrom semanticscholar import SemanticScholar\nsch = SemanticScholar()\nresults = sch.search_paper(\"attention mechanism transformers\", limit=5)\nfor p in results:\n    print(f\"{p.title} | DOI: {p.externalIds.get('DOI', 'N/A')}\")\n```\n\n### 3.4 引用处理规则\n\n| 情况 | 处理 |\n|------|------|\n| 找到论文 + DOI + BibTeX | 直接用 |\n| 找到论文，无 DOI | 用 arXiv BibTeX |\n| 论文存在但无法获取 BibTeX | 标记 placeholder，告知用户 |\n| 不确定论文是否存在 | 标记 `[CITATION NEEDED]`，告知用户 |\n| \"我觉得有篇关于 X 的论文\" | **绝不引用** — 先搜索或标记 |\n\n---\n\n## 4. 顶会模板速查\n\n### 4.1 ML/AI 会议\n\n| 会议 | 页数限制 | 主文件 | 样式文件 | 必须项 |\n|------|---------|--------|---------|--------|\n| **NeurIPS** | 9 页 | `main.tex` | `neurips.sty` | Paper Checklist |\n| **ICML** | 8 页 (+1 camera-ready) | `example_paper.tex` | `icml2026.sty` | Broader Impact |\n| **ICLR** | 9 页 (+1) | `iclr_conference.tex` | `iclr_conference.sty` | LLM 使用声明 |\n| **ACL/EMNLP** | 8 页 (long) | `acl_latex.tex` | `acl.sty` | Limitations 必须 |\n| **AAAI** | 7 页 (+1) | `aaai_template.tex` | `aaai.sty` | 严格样式 |\n| **COLM** | 9 页 (+1) | `colm_conference.tex` | `colm_conference.sty` | 语言模型聚焦 |\n\n### 4.2 系统会议\n\n| 会议 | 页数限制 | 模板类型 | 关键要求 |\n|------|---------|---------|---------|\n| **OSDI** | 12 页 (+2) | USENIX `usenix.sty` | Research / Operational 双 track |\n| **NSDI** | 12 页 | USENIX | Introduction 预筛 |\n| **ASPLOS** | 12 页 | ACM `acmart.cls (sigplan)` | 前 2 页快速评审 |\n| **SOSP** | 12 页 | ACM `acmart.cls (sigplan)` | 可选 Artifact Evaluation |\n\n### 4.3 模板使用原则\n\n```bash\n# 1. 复制整个模板目录（不要只拷 .tex）\ncp -r templates/neurips2025/ ~/papers/my-paper/\n\n# 2. 先编译未修改的模板，确认能跑通\nlatexmk -pdf main.tex\n\n# 3. 逐章替换内容，频繁编译\n```\n\n**禁止事项：**\n- 不要修改 `.sty` 文件\n- 不要在会议模板间复制 `\\usepackage` 行\n- 不要只复制 `.tex` 而不带 `.sty/.bst`\n\n---\n\n## 5. 写作规范\n\n### 5.1 时间分配（Neel Nanda）\n\n花**大致相等**的时间在：\n1. Abstract\n2. Introduction\n3. 图表\n4. **其余所有内容加起来**\n\n审稿人读论文的顺序：标题 → 摘要 → 引言 → 图表 → 也许正文。\n\n### 5.2 Abstract 五句法（Sebastian Farquhar）\n\n```\n1. 你做了什么（\"We introduce/prove/demonstrate...\"）\n2. 为什么这很难且重要\n3. 怎么做的（含专业关键词以便检索）\n4. 有什么证据\n5. 最惊人的数字/结果\n```\n\n删除一切泛泛的开头：~~\"Large language models have achieved remarkable success...\"~~\n\n### 5.3 句子级清晰度（Gopen & Swan）\n\n| 原则 | 规则 |\n|------|------|\n| 主谓紧邻 | 不要在主语和动词之间插入长从句 |\n| 重点在句尾 | 把关键数字/结论放在句末 |\n| 旧信息在前 | 先放读者已知的内容，再引入新概念 |\n| 动词表达动作 | \"We analyzed\" 而非 \"We performed an analysis\" |\n\n### 5.4 常见修改\n\n| 问题 | 修复 |\n|------|------|\n| Abstract 太泛 | 删第一句（如果它能放在任何 ML 论文前面） |\n| Introduction 超 1.5 页 | 背景挪到 Related Work |\n| 实验没有显式 claim | 每个实验前加 \"This tests whether...\" |\n| 缺统计显著性 | 加 error bars + 运行次数 + 计算方法 |\n\n---\n\n## 6. 投稿工程链\n\n### 6.1 构建\n\n```bash\n# 推荐：latexmk 一键构建\nlatexmk -pdf -interaction=nonstopmode -halt-on-error main.tex\n\n# 手动：需要多次编译\npdflatex main.tex && bibtex main && pdflatex main.tex && pdflatex main.tex\n```\n\n### 6.2 Lint + 格式化\n\n```bash\n# Lint: 检查常见 LaTeX 问题\nchktex main.tex\n\n# 格式化: 减少 diff 噪声\nlatexindent -w main.tex\n```\n\n### 6.3 图表格式\n\n| 类型 | 格式 | 说明 |\n|------|------|------|\n| 矢量图（plot、diagram） | PDF | matplotlib: `plt.savefig('fig.pdf')` |\n| 照片/heatmap | PNG 600dpi 或 TIFF | 栅格图 |\n| 表格 | `booktabs` 包 | `\\toprule/\\midrule/\\bottomrule` |\n\n```latex\n\\usepackage{booktabs}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & Accuracy $\\uparrow$ & Latency $\\downarrow$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nBaseline & 85.2 & 45ms \\\\\n\\textbf{Ours} & \\textbf{92.1} & 38ms \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n```\n\n**图表规范：**\n- 用色盲友好色板（Okabe-Ito 或 Paul Tol）\n- 图内不放标题（用 caption）\n- Caption 独立可理解（不依赖正文）\n- 最佳值加粗，标注方向符号（↑ 越高越好，↓ 越低越好）\n\n### 6.4 投稿打包\n\n```bash\n# 清理辅助文件\nlatexmk -c\n\n# 打包投稿\nzip -r submission.zip main.tex *.sty *.bst *.bib figures/ --exclude='*.aux' --exclude='*.log'\n```\n\n### 6.5 常见失败点\n\n| 问题 | 解决 |\n|------|------|\n| 图片格式混用导致编译失败 | 统一用 PDF |\n| 字体未嵌入 | `pdffonts output.pdf` 检查 |\n| Overfull box 过多 | 关键位置手动调行 |\n| 引用缺字段 | 检查 DOI/年份/期刊名 |\n| 超页数 | 用 `\\vspace{-Xpt}` 微调，或精简内容 |\n\n---\n\n## 7. 会议转投\n\n当论文被拒或转投其他会议时：\n\n### 7.1 ML/AI 会议间转换\n\n| 转换 | 页数变化 | 关键调整 |\n|------|---------|---------|\n| NeurIPS → ICML | 9→8 | 删 1 页，加 Broader Impact |\n| ICML → ICLR | 8→9 | 可扩展实验 + LLM 声明 |\n| NeurIPS → ACL | 9→8 | 按 NLP 惯例重组 + Limitations |\n| ICLR → AAAI | 9→7 | 大幅精简，严格样式 |\n\n### 7.2 转投原则\n\n1. **永远用目标模板重新开始** — 复制目标模板，迁移内容，不要合并 preamble\n2. **只迁移正文内容** — 不复制 `\\usepackage`、header/footer 命令\n3. **根据审稿意见改进** — 但不要在新稿中提及旧投稿（双盲）\n4. **检查目标会议特殊要求** — checklist、ethics statement、LLM 声明等\n\n---\n\n## 8. 投前清单\n\n### 通用清单\n\n- [ ] Abstract/Intro/Conclusion 的 claim 一致\n- [ ] 每个 contribution 有对应 evidence\n- [ ] 所有引用已程序化验证（无幻觉引用）\n- [ ] Error bars + 运行次数 + 统计方法\n- [ ] Limitations 章节存在且诚实\n- [ ] 匿名化（双盲审稿）\n- [ ] 符合页数限制\n- [ ] 所有图表有自包含 caption\n- [ ] `.bib` 中无缺失字段\n- [ ] PDF 编译无 warning\n\n### 会议特定\n\n| 会议 | 额外要求 |\n|------|---------|\n| NeurIPS | 16 项 Paper Checklist（附录，不计页数） |\n| ICML | Broader Impact Statement |\n| ICLR | LLM 使用声明 |\n| ACL/EMNLP | Limitations 章节（必须），Ethics Statement |\n| AAAI | 严格样式文件合规 |\n\n---\n\n## 参考资源\n\n**写作方法论：**\n- [Neel Nanda: How to Write ML Papers](https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/eJGptPbbFPZGLpjsp/) — What/Why/So What 框架\n- [Farquhar: How to Write ML ```text
1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	ContamCal operates in two stages: Stage~1 estimates per-item contamination exposure from multiple detection signals via training-free fusion; Stage~2 embeds these scores in a contamination-augmented IRT model to produce calibrated ability estimates with credible intervals.
5	
6	\subsection{Problem Formulation}
7	\label{sec:formulation}
8	
9	Let $y_{ijk} \in \{0,1\}$ denote the binary response of model~$j$ ($j=1,\ldots,J$) to item~$i$ ($i=1,\ldots,I_k$) on benchmark~$k$. Standard IRT assumes $P(y_{ijk}=1) = f(\theta_j, \boldsymbol{\xi}_i)$ where $\theta_j$ is latent ability and $\boldsymbol{\xi}_i$ collects item parameters. When training corpora contain benchmark items, the observed ability $\hat{\theta}_j$ confounds genuine capability with memorization.
10	
11	Our goal is twofold: (1)~estimate $\theta_j^{\text{clean}}$, the ability each model would exhibit had it never encountered test items during training, and (2)~diagnose contamination effects at domain granularity within each benchmark. We draw on item compromise IRT \citep{eckerly2017,sinharay2017} and explanatory IRT \citep{deboeck2004}, adapting these frameworks through three structural modifications for LLM evaluation.
12	
13	\textit{Adaptation~1: Independent examinees $\to$ shared item pool.} Classical IRT assumes independent test-takers. In LLM evaluation, a fixed item set is shared across all models, and responses within a single model may exhibit residual correlations beyond the scalar~$\theta_j$ \citep{sanmartin2009}. We mitigate this by fitting per-benchmark models.
14	
15	\textit{Adaptation~2: Binary leakage $\to$ continuous exposure.} Item compromise IRT uses binary indicators \citep{sinharay2017}. LLM contamination is graded: a model may have encountered an item zero, once, or hundreds of times. We replace binary indicators with continuous exposure scores $e_{ij} \in [0,1]$ derived from detection signals, following the explanatory IRT tradition \citep{deboeck2004}.
16	
17	\textit{Adaptation~3: Stable ability $\to$ regime-conditional ability.} In educational testing, ability is approximately constant. For LLMs, the training regime (SFT vs.\ RL) qualitatively changes both internal representations and signal reliability. We recover clean ability via counterfactual inference (setting exposure to zero) rather than regime-specific ability parameters.
18	
19	\subsection{Stage 1: Multi-Signal Exposure Mapping}
20	\label{sec:stage1}
21	
22	For each model~$j$ and item~$i$, we compute four complementary detection signals. \textbf{S1}~(Embedding similarity): cosine similarity between the item embedding and its nearest neighbor in the SFT training corpus. \textbf{S2}~(Min-K\%++): a membership inference statistic based on token-level surprisal \citep{duan2025minkpp}. \textbf{S3}~(ConStat): the accuracy gap between original and paraphrased item variants \citep{dekoninck2024constat}. \textbf{S4}~(Self-Critique): negative entropy of model self-evaluation distributions \citep{wust2025selfcritique}. S2 and S4 exhibit known failure modes under RL post-training, which we characterize in \S\ref{sec:signal_analysis}.
23	
24	We aggregate signals via Reciprocal Rank Fusion \citep[RRF;][]{cormack2009}:
25	\begin{equation}
26	\label{eq:rrf}
27	    e_{ij} = \sum_{s=1}^{4} \frac{1}{k + r_{s}(i)},
28	\end{equation}
29	where $r_s(i)$ is the rank of item~$i$ under signal~$s$ (rank~1 = most suspicious) and $k=60$ is the standard smoothing constant. Raw scores are min-max normalized per model to obtain $e_{ij} \in [0,1]$.
30	
31	RRF is a deliberate design choice. A learned aggregator requires labeled contamination data, but ground-truth labels are precisely what we lack. The aggregator's own training data may be contaminated, creating a meta-circularity problem. RRF sidesteps this by operating on rank orderings without parameter estimation.
32	
33	\subsection{Stage 2: Contamination-Augmented IRT}
34	\label{sec:stage2}
35	
36	\paragraph{Primary model: 2PL+$\gamma$.}
37	Our primary specification augments the two-parameter logistic model with a per-domain contamination covariate. For benchmark~$k$ containing domains $d \in \{1,\ldots,D_k\}$ (e.g., MMLU has $D_k=8$ subject domains), the response probability is:
38	\begin{equation}
39	\label{eq:2pl_gamma}
40	    P(y_{ijk} = 1) = \sigma\!\bigl(a_i(\theta_j - b_i) + \gamma_{d(i)} \cdot e_{ij}\bigr),
41	\end{equation}
42	where $\sigma(\cdot)$ is the logistic function, $a_i > 0$ is item discrimination, $b_i \in \mathbb{R}$ is item difficulty, $\theta_j$ is model ability, and $\gamma_{d(i)}$ is the contamination effect for the domain containing item~$i$. The per-domain specification permits heterogeneous effects: memorization may inflate knowledge recall while leaving reasoning items unaffected within the same benchmark. When a benchmark lacks domain structure, a single $\gamma_k$ is estimated.
43	
44	The additive structure admits a natural interpretation: contamination shifts the effective difficulty of item~$i$ by $\gamma_{d(i)} \cdot e_{ij} / a_i$ units on the ability scale.
45	
46	\paragraph{4PL extension for multiple-choice benchmarks.}
47	For MC-format benchmarks (ARC, MMLU) where guessing and ceiling effects are non-negligible, we extend to:
48	\begin{equation}
49	\label{eq:4pl_gamma}
50	    P(y_{ijk} = 1) = c_i + (d_i - c_i)\,\sigma\!\bigl(a_i(\theta_j - b_i) + \gamma_{d(i)} \cdot e_{ij}\bigr),
51	\end{equation}
52	with guessing asymptote $c_i \sim \text{Beta}(5,20)$ and ceiling $d_i \sim \text{Beta}(20,5)$. This extension requires $J \geq 16$ models to avoid overparameterization; with fewer respondents, the $c_i$ and $d_i$ parameters become unidentifiable (empirically confirmed: ARC with $J=5$ under 4PL+$\gamma$ yields PPP$=$0.000 and MAE degradation from 0.004 to 0.068).
53	
54	\paragraph{Inference.}
55	We perform Bayesian inference via NUTS \citep{hoffman2014} in Stan \citep{carpenter2017}, running four chains with target acceptance 0.95. Priors: $\theta_j \sim \mathcal{N}(0,1)$, $a_i \sim \text{LogNormal}(0, 0.5)$, $b_i \sim \mathcal{N}(0,1)$, $\gamma_{d} \sim \mathcal{N}(0,1)$. Convergence requires split-$\hat{R} < 1.01$ and bulk ESS~$> 400$ for all parameters.
56	
57	\paragraph{Calibrated score.}
58	The decontaminated accuracy for model~$j$ on benchmark~$k$ is the posterior expectation under zero exposure:
59	\begin{equation}
60	\label{eq:calibrated}
61	    \text{Acc}_{j}^{\text{cal}} = \frac{1}{I_k} \sum_{i=1}^{I_k}\, \mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\psi} \sim p(\boldsymbol{\psi} \mid \mathbf{y})} \!\left[\sigma\!\bigl(a_i(\theta_j - b_i)\bigr)\right],
62	\end{equation}
63	with credible intervals from posterior quantiles. Models with noisier exposure signals receive wider intervals by construction.
64	
65	\subsection{Fisher Information and Identifiability}
66	\label{sec:fisher}
67	
68	The reliability of $\gamma$ estimation depends on two factors: signal discriminative quality (AUC of exposure scores) and ensemble size~$J$. The expected Fisher information $\mathcal{I}(\gamma)$ depends on $\text{Var}(e_{\cdot i})$ across models for each item (full derivation in Appendix~A). When signal AUC is low, exposure scores become uniform, collapsing this variance and rendering $\gamma$ structurally unidentifiable.
69	
70	We validate via simulation across 24 conditions (4~AUC levels $\times$ 4~ensemble sizes, 10 seeds each). Recovery succeeds down to AUC$=$0.55 (lowest tested) with bias~$<0.05$ and 95\% CI coverage~$\geq$90\% at $J \geq 50$. Posterior CI width scales as $J^{-0.48}$, close to the parametric $J^{-0.5}$ rate.
71	
72	This reveals a domain-method mismatch: IRT-based LLM evaluation typically pools $J = 8$--$30$ models \citep{polo2026growing,lalor2024irt}, well below the $J \geq 50$ regime where $\gamma$ recovery is reliable. This structural gap is itself a central finding (\S\ref{sec:results}).
73	
74	\subsection{Calibration Value Decomposition}
75	\label{sec:value_decomposition}
76	
77	Eq.~\eqref{eq:calibrated} depends on the ability estimate~$\theta_j$, not on $\gamma$ directly. Two mechanisms contribute to calibration quality: (a)~IRT ability estimation, which separates stable capability from item-level noise regardless of the contamination model; and (b)~the explicit $\gamma$ correction, which absorbs contamination variance during joint estimation of~$\theta_j$.
78	
79	Empirically, vanilla 2PL ($\gamma\!=\!0$) achieves calibration MAE comparable to 2PL+$\gamma$ across all benchmarks: GSM8K 0.0063 vs 0.0062, MMLU 0.0068 vs 0.0070, ARC 0.0158 vs 0.0201. The calibration benefit comes entirely from IRT ability estimation itself, which provides better $\theta$ separation from noise than raw accuracy.
80

      "content": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nContamCal operates in two stages: Stage~1 estimates per-item contamination exposure from multiple detection signals via training-free fusion; Stage~2 embeds these scores in a contamination-augmented IRT model to produce calibrated ability estimates with credible intervals.\n\n\\subsection{Problem Formulation}\n\\label{sec:formulation}\n\nLet $y_{ijk} \\in \\{0,1\\}$ denote the binary response of model~$j$ ($j=1,\\ldots,J$) to item~$i$ ($i=1,\\ldots,I_k$) on benchmark~$k$. Standard IRT assumes $P(y_{ijk}=1) = f(\\theta_j, \\boldsymbol{\\xi}_i)$ where $\\theta_j$ is latent ability and $\\boldsymbol{\\xi}_i$ collects item parameters. When training corpora contain benchmark items, the observed ability $\\hat{\\theta}_j$ confounds genuine capability with memorization.\n\nOur goal is twofold: (1)~estimate $\\theta_j^{\\text{clean}}$, the ability each model would exhibit had it never encountered test items during training, and (2)~diagnose contamination effects at domain granularity within each benchmark. We draw on item compromise IRT \\citep{eckerly2017,sinharay2017} and explanatory IRT \\citep{deboeck2004}, adapting these frameworks through three structural modifications for LLM evaluation.\n\n\\textit{Adaptation~1: Independent examinees $\\to$ shared item pool.} Classical IRT assumes independent test-takers. In LLM evaluation, a fixed item set is shared across all models, and responses within a single model may exhibit residual correlations beyond the scalar~$\\theta_j$ \\citep{sanmartin2009}. We mitigate this by fitting per-benchmark models.\n\n\\textit{Adaptation~2: Binary leakage $\\to$ continuous exposure.} Item compromise IRT uses binary indicators \\citep{sinharay2017}. LLM contamination is graded: a model may have encountered an item zero, once, or hundreds of times. We replace binary indicators with continuous exposure scores $e_{ij} \\in [0,1]$ derived from detection signals, following the explanatory IRT tradition \\citep{deboeck2004}.\n\n\\textit{Adaptation~3: Stable ability $\\to$ regime-conditional ability.} In educational testing, ability is approximately constant. For LLMs, the training regime (SFT vs.\\ RL) qualitatively changes both internal representations and signal reliability. We recover clean ability via counterfactual inference (setting exposure to zero) rather than regime-specific ability parameters.\n\n\\subsection{Stage 1: Multi-Signal Exposure Mapping}\n\\label{sec:stage1}\n\nFor each model~$j$ and item~$i$, we compute four complementary detection signals. \\textbf{S1}~(Embedding similarity): cosine similarity between the item embedding and its nearest neighbor in the SFT training corpus. \\textbf{S2}~(Min-K\\%++): a membership inference statistic based on token-level surprisal \\citep{duan2025minkpp}. \\textbf{S3}~(ConStat): the accuracy gap between original and paraphrased item variants \\citep{dekoninck2024constat}. \\textbf{S4}~(Self-Critique): negative entropy of model self-evaluation distributions \\citep{wust2025selfcritique}. S2 and S4 exhibit known failure modes under RL post-training, which we characterize in \\S\\ref{sec:signal_analysis}.\n\nWe aggregate signals via Reciprocal Rank Fusion \\citep[RRF;][]{cormack2009}:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:rrf}\n    e_{ij} = \\sum_{s=1}^{4} \\frac{1}{k + r_{s}(i)},\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $r_s(i)$ is the rank of item~$i$ under signal~$s$ (rank~1 = most suspicious) and $k=60$ is the standard smoothing constant. Raw scores are min-max normalized per model to obtain $e_{ij} \\in [0,1]$.\n\nRRF is a deliberate design choice. A learned aggregator requires labeled contamination data, but ground-truth labels are precisely what we lack. The aggregator's own training data may be contaminated, creating a meta-circularity problem. RRF sidesteps this by operating on rank orderings without parameter estimation.\n\n\\subsection{Stage 2: Contamination-Augmented IRT}\n\\label{sec:stage2}\n\n\\paragraph{Primary model: 2PL+$\\gamma$.}\nOur primary specification augments the two-parameter logistic model with a per-domain contamination covariate. For benchmark~$k$ containing domains $d \\in \\{1,\\ldots,D_k\\}$ (e.g., MMLU has $D_k=8$ subject domains), the response probability is:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:2pl_gamma}\n    P(y_{ijk} = 1) = \\sigma\\!\\bigl(a_i(\\theta_j - b_i) + \\gamma_{d(i)} \\cdot e_{ij}\\bigr),\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\sigma(\\cdot)$ is the logistic function, $a_i > 0$ is item discrimination, $b_i \\in \\mathbb{R}$ is item difficulty, $\\theta_j$ is model ability, and $\\gamma_{d(i)}$ is the contamination effect for the domain containing item~$i$. The per-domain specification permits heterogeneous effects: memorization may inflate knowledge recall while leaving reasoning items unaffected within the same benchmark. When a benchmark lacks domain structure, a single $\\gamma_k$ is estimated.\n\nThe additive structure admits a natural interpretation: contamination shifts the effective difficulty of item~$i$ by $\\gamma_{d(i)} \\cdot e_{ij} / a_i$ units on the ability scale.\n\n\\paragraph{4PL extension for multiple-choice benchmarks.}\nFor MC-format benchmarks (ARC, MMLU) where guessing and ceiling effects are non-negligible, we extend to:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:4pl_gamma}\n    P(y_{ijk} = 1) = c_i + (d_i - c_i)\\,\\sigma\\!\\bigl(a_i(\\theta_j - b_i) + \\gamma_{d(i)} \\cdot e_{ij}\\bigr),\n\\end{equation}\nwith guessing asymptote $c_i \\sim \\text{Beta}(5,20)$ and ceiling $d_i \\sim \\text{Beta}(20,5)$. This extension requires $J \\geq 16$ models to avoid overparameterization; with fewer respondents, the $c_i$ and $d_i$ parameters become unidentifiable (empirically confirmed: ARC with $J=5$ under 4PL+$\\gamma$ yields PPP$=$0.000 and MAE degradation from 0.004 to 0.068).\n\n\\paragraph{Inference.}\nWe perform Bayesian inference via NUTS \\citep{hoffman2014} in Stan \\citep{carpenter2017}, running four chains with target acceptance 0.95. Priors: $\\theta_j \\sim \\mathcal{N}(0,1)$, $a_i \\sim \\text{LogNormal}(0, 0.5)$, $b_i \\sim \\mathcal{N}(0,1)$, $\\gamma_{d} \\sim \\mathcal{N}(0,1)$. Convergence requires split-$\\hat{R} < 1.01$ and bulk ESS~$> 400$ for all parameters.\n\n\\paragraph{Calibrated score.}\nThe decontaminated accuracy for model~$j$ on benchmark~$k$ is the posterior expectation under zero exposure:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:calibrated}\n    \\text{Acc}_{j}^{\\text{cal}} = \\frac{1}{I_k} \\sum_{i=1}^{I_k}\\, \\mathbb{E}_{\\boldsymbol{\\psi} \\sim p(\\boldsymbol{\\psi} \\mid \\mathbf{y})} \\!\\left[\\sigma\\!\\bigl(a_i(\\theta_j - b_i)\\bigr)\\right],\n\\end{equation}\nwith credible intervals from posterior quantiles. Models with noisier exposure signals receive wider intervals by construction.\n\n\\subsection{Fisher Information and Identifiability}\n\\label{sec:fisher}\n\nThe reliability of $\\gamma$ estimation depends on two factors: signal discriminative quality (AUC of exposure scores) and ensemble size~$J$. The expected Fisher information $\\mathcal{I}(\\gamma)$ depends on $\\text{Var}(e_{\\cdot i})$ across models for each item (full derivation in Appendix~A). When signal AUC is low, exposure scores become uniform, collapsing this variance and rendering $\\gamma$ structurally unidentifiable.\n\nWe validate via simulation across 24 conditions (4~AUC levels $\\times$ 4~ensemble sizes, 10 seeds each). Recovery succeeds down to AUC$=$0.55 (lowest tested) with bias~$<0.05$ and 95\\% CI coverage~$\\geq$90\\% at $J \\geq 50$. Posterior CI width scales as $J^{-0.48}$, close to the parametric $J^{-0.5}$ rate.\n\nThis reveals a domain-method mismatch: IRT-based LLM evaluation typically pools $J = 8$--$30$ models \\citep{polo2026growing,lalor2024irt}, well below the $J \\geq 50$ regime where $\\gamma$ recovery is reliable. Two mechanisms contribute to calibration quality: (a)~IRT ability estimation, which separates stable capability from item-level noise regardless of the contamination model; and (b)~the explicit $\\gamma$ correction, which absorbs contamination variance during joint estimation of~$\\theta_j$.\n\nEmpirically, vanilla 2PL ($\\gamma\\!=\\!0$) achieves calibration MAE comparable to 2PL+$\\gamma$ across all benchmarks: GSM8K 0.0063 vs 0.0062, MMLU 0.0068 vs 0.0070, ARC 0.0158 vs 0.0201. ```text
1	\section{Results}
2	\label{sec:results}
3	
4	We organize results around our four contributions. Section~\ref{sec:domain_differential} presents the flagship finding: domain-level contamination heterogeneity within MMLU that pooled analysis completely obscures. Section~\ref{sec:signal_failure} characterizes detection signal failure modes, including a directional reversal in Min-K\%++ at small model scales. Section~\ref{sec:identifiability} quantifies the structural identifiability boundaries of IRT-based contamination estimation. Section~\ref{sec:calibration_results} reports calibration quality and clarifies the value decomposition between IRT ability estimation and the contamination parameter.
5	
6	\subsection{Domain-Differential Contamination}
7	\label{sec:domain_differential}
8	
9	\paragraph{Within-benchmark heterogeneity.}
10	Table~\ref{tab:domain_gamma} and Figure~\ref{fig:domain_gamma} present per-domain $\gamma$ estimates from the 2PL+$\gamma$ model fitted on the $J=24$ Qwen2.5 ensemble (exp-006). MMLU exhibits striking heterogeneity across its eight cognitive domains. Math ($\gamma = +0.97$, 95\% CI $[0.53, 1.43]$, PPP$=0.44$) and Science ($\gamma = +0.84$, CI $[0.39, 1.27]$, PPP$=0.81$) show strong contamination inflation with credible intervals excluding zero and acceptable model fit. CS \& Engineering ($\gamma = +0.20$, CI $[-0.45, 0.84]$) shows a positive point estimate that does not reach significance.
11	
12	The remaining five domains show null or negative $\gamma$ estimates: Business \& Law ($-0.17$), Medical ($-0.20$), Humanities ($-0.41$), and Social Sciences ($-0.50$), all with PPP$=1.00$ indicating model misfit. Negative $\gamma$ values with PPP$=1.00$ do not indicate that contamination reduces performance; rather, they signal that the 2PL+$\gamma$ specification does not capture the response pattern in these domains. One plausible explanation: these domains contain items where memorized surface patterns conflict with reasoning requirements, producing a non-monotone relationship between exposure and correctness that a single linear $\gamma$ cannot represent.
13	
14	\paragraph{Dilution masking.}
15	The pooled MMLU estimate ($\gamma_{\text{pooled}} = +0.66$, CI $[-0.41, 1.74]$) fails to reach significance. This is not evidence of absent contamination effects. Positive STEM-domain effects are arithmetically cancelled by null and negative non-STEM estimates when aggregated, producing a near-zero pooled value. This phenomenon parallels Simpson's paradox \citep{kievit_2013_simpsons_paradox} and DIF cancellation in educational measurement \citep{ercikan_2008_dif_cancellation}: subgroup effects of opposite sign cancel at the aggregate level, creating a misleading impression of no effect. Domain-level analysis is therefore essential for contamination diagnosis; pooled estimates will systematically understate the problem when contamination is heterogeneous.
16	
17	\paragraph{Cross-benchmark comparison.}
18	ARC-Challenge yields $\gamma = -1.46$ with a credible interval excluding zero, indicating that contaminated models perform \textit{worse} on ARC items after controlling for ability. This ``contamination forgetting'' pattern is consistent with SFT injection disrupting the reasoning strategies required for science questions. GSM8K shows $\gamma = +0.06$ (CI includes zero, PPP$=0.54$), a non-significant effect; however, GSM8K was evaluated with S1 (embedding similarity) as the only reliable signal for free-form generation items, limiting the exposure score discriminability.
19	
20	\paragraph{Dose-response structure.}
21	Figure~\ref{fig:dose_response} visualizes the relationship between injection dosage and raw accuracy across scales. The pattern is monotonically increasing for MMLU STEM domains but shows diminishing returns above d025. At the 0.5B scale, contamination effects are catastrophic (accuracy jumps from 0.25 to 0.60 at d100 for Math items); at 3B, the same dosage produces a mild 3-5 percentage point increase. This scale-dependent attenuation is consistent with larger models having greater capacity to absorb injected items without disrupting learned representations.
22	
23	% Cross-family analysis
24	\textit{Cross-family evidence.}
25	[PLACEHOLDER: pending s1 fix for full cross-family pooled IRT results.
26	Current v2 data (Llama-3.2-3B d000 only): ARC $\gamma=0.554$, CI$_{95}$=$[-0.121, 1.228]$; GSM8K $\gamma=-0.010$, CI$_{95}$=$[-0.441, 0.413]$.
27	Weak claim only: IRT calibration is robust to inclusion of a new model family, and $\gamma$ estimates remain consistent with within-family patterns.]
28	
29	\subsection{Detection Signal Failure Modes}
30	\label{sec:signal_failure}
31	
32	\paragraph{Min-K\%++ directional reversal.}
33	The most consequential finding for contamination detection practice is that Min-K\%++ \citep{zhang_2024_minkpp} exhibits a \textit{directional reversal} at the 0.5B scale (exp-008). Contaminated items receive \textit{lower} Min-K\%++ scores than clean items, yielding Cohen's $d = -1.55$ at d005 and $d = -1.98$ at d100 relative to d000. A practitioner applying Min-K\%++ at this scale would conclude that contaminated items are \textit{less} likely to be memorized. The signal is not merely ineffective; it is actively misleading.
34	
35	At 1.5B, Min-K\%++ shows mild positive discrimination ($d \approx +0.3$); at 3B, moderate positive discrimination ($d \approx +0.7$). The transition from negative to positive discrimination occurs between 0.5B and 1.5B parameters. We hypothesize that SFT injection at 0.5B produces a distributional shift in the token-level surprisal landscape: the model's language modeling capacity is so disrupted that \textit{all} items exhibit high surprisal, but contaminated items (now overfit at the response level) paradoxically show reduced token-level fluency compared to clean items on which the model retains pretraining distributions.
36	
37	\paragraph{Signal reliability across scales.}
38	Figure~\ref{fig:signal_heatmap} summarizes detection performance (AUC) across all signal-scale-dosage combinations. S1 (embedding similarity) achieves AUC $> 0.95$ at d005 and above for all three model scales, confirming its reliability as a standalone signal when training corpus access is available. S4 (Self-Critique) \citep{wust_2025_selfcritique} performs near-random at 0.5B (AUC $\approx 0.50$) and reaches marginal discrimination at 1.5B and 3B. RRF fusion, which assumes complementary signal contributions, degrades to S1-only performance in practice because S2 and S4 contribute noise rather than signal at small scales.
39	
40	% Cross-family signal
41	\textit{Cross-family signal behavior.}
42	[PLACEHOLDER: pending s1 fix for full cross-family signal comparison. Preliminary: Llama-3.2-3B also exhibits Min-K\%++ reversal direction at comparable scales, suggesting this is a model-scale phenomenon rather than architecture-specific.]
43	
44	\subsection{Identifiability Boundaries}
45	\label{sec:identifiability}
46	
47	The simulation study (exp-004; 24 conditions, 4 AUC levels $\times$ 4 ensemble sizes $\times$ 2 contamination strengths, 10 seeds each = 240 runs) establishes the operating envelope for IRT-based contamination estimation. Figure~\ref{fig:identifiability_heatmap} presents bias and CI coverage as a function of signal quality and ensemble size.
48	
49	\paragraph{Recovery thresholds.}
50	Parameter recovery succeeds (bias $< 0.05$, 95\% CI coverage $\geq 90\%$) at signal AUC as low as 0.55 (the minimum tested), provided $J \geq 50$ models are available. Posterior CI width decreases as $J^{-0.48}$, close to the parametric $J^{-0.5}$ rate. At $J = 24$ (our empirical ensemble size), recovery remains adequate for domains with AUC $\geq 0.70$, but CI widths increase by 40\% relative to $J = 50$.
51	
52	\paragraph{Domain-method mismatch.}
53	Current IRT-based LLM evaluation operates at $J = 8$--$30$ models \citep{polo2026growing, lalor2024irt}. This is well below the $J \geq 50$ regime where $\gamma$ recovery is most reliable. The constraint is not a fundamental limitation of the IRT framework; it reflects the practical reality that LLM evaluation pools far fewer ``respondents'' than educational testing (where $J > 1000$ is typical). Signal quality becomes the binding constraint: when detection AUC is high ($\geq 0.90$, as S1 achieves), even $J = 16$ produces reliable $\gamma$ estimates. The identifiability boundary is therefore a joint function of $J$ and signal AUC, not either alone.
54	
55	\subsection{Calibration Quality}
56	\label{sec:calibration_results}
57	
58	\paragraph{Value decomposition.}
59	Table~\ref{tab:baselines} compares calibration MAE across methods. The key finding is that vanilla 2PL without a contamination parameter (B3, $\gamma = 0$) achieves calibration performance comparable to the full 2PL+$\gamma$ model across all benchmarks: ARC MAE 0.016 vs.\ 0.020, GSM8K MAE 0.006 vs.\ 0.006, MMLU MAE 0.007 vs.\ 0.007. The calibration benefit comes entirely from IRT ability estimation itself, not from the explicit $\gamma$ correction. IRT separates stable capability from item-level noise through its measurement model, producing better-calibrated $\theta$ estimates than raw accuracy regardless of whether contamination is explicitly modeled.
60	
61	This result does \textit{not} undermine the ContamCal framework. The $\gamma$ parameters serve a diagnostic role distinct from calibration: they reveal \textit{which} domains exhibit inflation or deflation, the direction and magnitude of effects, and model misfit patterns. Table~\ref{tab:domain_gamma} delivers actionable information (``MMLU Math is inflated by $\sim$1 logit'') that no calibration metric can provide. Calibration answers ``how much to trust the score''; domain-level $\gamma$ answers ``where and why the score is distorted.''
62	
63	\paragraph{Gating and negative results.}
64	ARC passes the calibration gating criteria: MAE reduction of 0.014 relative to B0-Raw (0.034 $\to$ 0.020) and a $\gamma$ estimate with CI excluding zero ($\tau = 0.756$). GSM8K fails gating because $\gamma$ is non-significant; the IRT model provides calibration improvement over raw scores but cannot claim contamination-specific correction. We also report two honest negatives. First, credible interval coverage falls below nominal levels (50\% CI achieves $\sim$35\% empirical coverage), indicating that the posterior intervals function as directional indicators rather than precise probability statements. Second, the GSM8K overcorrection ratio is 11$\times$: the model corrects 11 units of noise for every 1 unit of genuine contamination effect, a consequence of the non-significant $\gamma$ being applied uniformly.
65	
66	\paragraph{Comparison to baselines.}
67	B0-Raw scores show substantial miscalibration on ARC (MAE$=$0.034) due to contamination-induced position bias effects, but minimal miscalibration on MMLU (MAE$=$0.016) where diverse domains partially cancel errors. B1-Remove\&Rescore reduces item count without improving calibration when contamination effects are heterogeneous across items. B4-LinearRegression achieves comparable MAE to IRT methods on GSM8K but fails on ARC where the nonlinear ability-difficulty interaction matters. B5-Emperor \citep{xu2025emperor} results confirm that mitigation-based approaches (rephrasing, option shuffling) do not restore clean performance, motivating the post-hoc calibration approach.
68	
69	\begin{table}[t]
70	\centering
71	\caption{Domain-level contamination parameters from 2PL+$\gamma$ (exp-006, $J$=24 Qwen2.5 models). Domains ordered by $\gamma$ magnitude. PPP values outside $[0.1, 0.9]$ indicate model misfit.}
72	\label{tab:domain_gamma}
73	\small

79	MMLU: Science & +0.84 [0.39, 1.27] & 0.81 & Inflation \\
80	MMLU: CS/Eng & +0.20 [$-$0.45, 0.84] & 0.89 & Non-sig. \\

This scale-dependent attenuation is consistent with larger models having greater capacity to absorb injected items without disrupting learned representations.\n\n% Cross-family analysis\n\\textit{Cross-family evidence.}\n[PLACEHOLDER: pending s1 fix for full cross-family pooled IRT results.\nCurrent v2 data (Llama-3.2-3B d000 only): ARC $\\gamma=0.554$, CI$_{95}$=$[-0.121, 1.228]$; GSM8K $\\gamma=-0.010$, CI$_{95}$=$[-0.441, 0.413]$.\nWeak claim only: IRT calibration is robust to inclusion of a new model family, and $\\gamma$ estimates remain consistent with within-family patterns.]\n\n\\subsection{Detection Signal Failure Modes}\n\\label{sec:signal_failure}\n\n\\paragraph{Min-K\\%++ directional reversal.}\nThe most consequential finding for contamination detection practice is that Min-K\\%++ \\citep{zhang_2024_minkpp} exhibits a \\textit{directional reversal} at the 0.5B scale (exp-008). Contaminated items receive \\textit{lower} Min-K\\%++ scores than clean items, yielding Cohen's $d = -1.55$ at d005 and $d = -1.98$ at d100 relative to d000. A practitioner applying Min-K\\%++ at this scale would conclude that contaminated items are \\textit{less} likely to be memorized. The signal is not merely ineffective; it is actively misleading.\n\nAt 1.5B, Min-K\\%++ shows mild positive discrimination ($d \\approx +0.3$); at 3B, moderate positive discrimination ($d \\approx +0.7$). The transition from negative to positive discrimination occurs between 0.5B and 1.5B parameters. We hypothesize that SFT injection at 0.5B produces a distributional shift in the token-level surprisal landscape: the model's language modeling capacity is so disrupted that \\textit{all} items exhibit high surprisal, but contaminated items (now overfit at the response level) paradoxically show reduced token-level fluency compared to clean items on which the model retains pretraining distributions.\n\n\\paragraph{Signal reliability across scales.}\nFigure~\\ref{fig:signal_heatmap} summarizes detection performance (AUC) across all signal-scale-dosage combinations. S1 (embedding similarity) achieves AUC $> 0.95$ at d005 and above for all three model scales, confirming its reliability as a standalone signal when training corpus access is available. S4 (Self-Critique) \\citep{wust_2025_selfcritique} performs near-random at 0.5B (AUC $\\approx 0.50$) and reaches marginal discrimination at 1.5B and 3B. RRF fusion, which assumes complementary signal contributions, degrades to S1-only performance in practice because S2 and S4 contribute noise rather than signal at small scales.\n\n% Cross-family signal\n\\textit{Cross-family signal behavior.}\n[PLACEHOLDER: pending s1 fix for full cross-family signal comparison. Preliminary: Llama-3.2-3B also exhibits Min-K\\%++ reversal direction at comparable scales, suggesting this is a model-scale phenomenon rather than architecture-specific.]\n\n\\subsection{Identifiability Boundaries}\n\\label{sec:identifiability}\n\nThe simulation study (exp-004; 24 conditions, 4 AUC levels $\\times$ 4 ensemble sizes $\\times$ 2 contamination strengths, 10 seeds each = 240 runs) establishes the operating envelope for IRT-based contamination estimation. Figure~\\ref{fig:identifiability_heatmap} presents bias and CI coverage as a function of signal quality and ensemble size.\n\n\\paragraph{Recovery thresholds.}\nParameter recovery succeeds (bias $< 0.05$, 95\\% CI coverage $\\geq 90\\%$) at signal AUC as low as 0.55 (the minimum tested), provided $J \\geq 50$ models are available. Posterior CI width decreases as $J^{-0.48}$, close to the parametric $J^{-0.5}$ rate. At $J = 24$ (our empirical ensemble size), recovery remains adequate for domains with AUC $\\geq 0.70$, but CI widths increase by 40\\% relative to $J = 50$.\n\n\\paragraph{Domain-method mismatch.}\nCurrent IRT-based LLM evaluation operates at $J = 8$--$30$ models \\citep{polo2026growing, lalor2024irt}. This is well below the $J \\geq 50$ regime where $\\gamma$ recovery is most reliable. The constraint is not a fundamental limitation of the IRT framework; it reflects the practical reality that LLM evaluation pools far fewer ``respondents'' than educational testing (where $J > 1000$ is typical). Signal quality becomes the binding constraint: when detection AUC is high ($\\geq 0.90$, as S1 achieves), even $J = 16$ produces reliable $\\gamma$ estimates. The identifiability boundary is therefore a joint function of $J$ and signal AUC, not either alone.\n\n\\subsection{Calibration Quality}\n\\label{sec:calibration_results}\n\n\\paragraph{Value decomposition.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:baselines} compares calibration MAE across methods. The key finding is that vanilla 2PL without a contamination parameter (B3, $\\gamma = 0$) achieves calibration performance comparable to the full 2PL+$\\gamma$ model across all benchmarks: ARC MAE 0.016 vs.\\ 0.020, GSM8K MAE 0.006 vs.\\ 0.006, MMLU MAE 0.007 vs.\\ 0.007. The calibration benefit comes entirely from IRT ability estimation itself, not from the explicit $\\gamma$ correction. IRT separates stable capability from item-level noise through its measurement model, producing better-calibrated $\\theta$ estimates than raw accuracy regardless of whether contamination is explicitly modeled.\n\nThis result does \\textit{not} undermine the ContamCal framework. The $\\gamma$ parameters serve a diagnostic role distinct from calibration: they reveal \\textit{which} domains exhibit inflation or deflation, the direction and magnitude of effects, and model misfit patterns. Table~\\ref{tab:domain_gamma} delivers actionable information (``MMLU Math is inflated by $\\sim$1 logit'') that no calibration metric can provide. Calibration answers ``how much to trust the score''; domain-level $\\gamma$ answers ``where and why the score is distorted.''\n\n\\paragraph{Gating and negative results.}\nARC passes the calibration gating criteria: MAE reduction of 0.014 relative to B0-Raw (0.034 $\\to$ 0.020) and a $\\gamma$ estimate with CI excluding zero ($\\tau = 0.756$). GSM8K fails gating because $\\gamma$ is non-significant; the IRT model provides calibration improvement over raw scores but cannot claim contamination-specific correction. We also report two honest negatives. First, credible interval coverage falls below nominal levels (50\\% CI achieves $\\sim$35\\% empirical coverage), indicating that the posterior intervals function as directional indicators rather than precise probability statements. Second, the GSM8K overcorrection ratio is 11$\\times$: the model corrects 11 units of noise for every 1 unit of genuine contamination effect, a consequence of the non-significant $\\gamma$ being applied uniformly.\n\n\\paragraph{Comparison to baselines.}\nB0-Raw scores show substantial miscalibration on ARC (MAE$=$0.034) due to contamination-induced position bias effects, but minimal miscalibration on MMLU (MAE$=$0.016) where diverse domains partially cancel errors. B1-Remove\\&Rescore reduces item count without improving calibration when contamination effects are heterogeneous across items. B4-LinearRegression achieves comparable MAE to IRT methods on GSM8K but fails on ARC where the nonlinear ability-difficulty interaction matters. B5-Emperor \\citep{xu2025emperor} results confirm that mitigation-based approaches (rephrasing, option shuffling) do not restore clean performance, motivating the post-hoc calibration approach.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Domain-level contamination parameters from 2PL+$\\gamma$ (exp-006, $J$=24 Qwen2.5 models). Domains ordered by $\\gamma$ magnitude. PPP values outside $[0.1, 0.9]$ indicate model misfit.}\n\\label{tab:domain_gamma}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nDomain & $\\gamma$ [95\\% CI] & PPP & Interpretation \\\\\n\\midrule\nMMLU: Math & +0.97 [0.53, 1.43] & 0.44 & Inflation \\\\\nMMLU: Science & +0.84 [0.39, 1.27] & 0.81 & Inflation \\\\\nMMLU: CS/Eng & +0.20 [$-$0.45, 0.84] & 0.89 & Non-sig. \\\\",

Let me draft the opening paragraph on the problem. The key insight is that contamination isn't just a binary issue—it varies across different types of test items within the same benchmark. MMLU might show strong inflation on math questions but minimal effects on humanities. Aggregate corrections that average across all items miss this nuance entirely.

Now I'm moving into why this matters urgently. Recent work by Xu and colleagues tested 20 different mitigation strategies and found none of them actually restore clean performance once contamination has already happened. That's a critical finding because it means the old approach of trying to prevent contamination no longer works for models trained on unrestricted web data. At the same time, open-weight models trained on massive internet corpora are now everywhere, making contamination nearly impossible to measure precisely at the individual instance level. New contamination-free benchmarks like LiveBench help but come with the tradeoff of losing the comparability and institutional knowledge we've built up around established benchmarks.

The field needs statistical tools that can work retroactively—tools that quantify contamination effects even when we can't detect it perfectly.

Existing detection methods like Min-K%++, ConStat, and Kernel Divergence Signatures can identify contaminated items but don't tell us how much the scores are actually inflated, where that inflation happens across domains, or whether it affects all test items uniformly or varies. Score adjustment approaches like DCR offer point corrections but lack uncertainty estimates and domain-level detail. IRT-based frameworks are starting to address this but haven't fully solved the problem.

I'm proposing ContamCal, a two-stage framework that combines contamination detection with IRT-based evaluation. The first stage aggregates multiple detection signals into continuous exposure scores using Reciprocal Rank Fusion, which avoids the circular problem of needing contamination labels to train a detector. The second stage incorporates these exposure scores into an IRT model that can estimate how contamination affects ability estimates across different domains.

What's striking is a pattern I'm calling dilution masking: within MMLU, Math and Science domains show substantial score inflation while the overall effect across all domains appears negligible because opposite effects in non-STEM areas cancel out the STEM inflation. This heterogeneity means aggregate-level contamination analysis can be deeply misleading when effects vary by domain.

Beyond that, I'm documenting how Min-K%++ actually reverses direction at smaller model scales below 1.5B parameters, making the detection signal actively harmful rather than just ineffective. And I'm establishing identifiability boundaries through Fisher Information analysis—showing that recovering reliable contamination estimates requires signal AUC above 0.55 and at least 50 models in an ensemble, which exposes a fundamental gap between what current LLM evaluation practices can actually support.

Finally, I'm finding that IRT-based ability estimation delivers calibration improvements regardless of the contamination parameter itself; instead, the contamination estimate functions as a diagnostic tool that pinpoints which domains are distorted and by what magnitude, rather than directly boosting score accuracy. Finished verifying all the citation keys against the bibliography—everything checks out. Now I'll write the file.

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_013moL7wZZ8ePpE67xHyCm2Y`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:intro}
3	
4	Large language model (LLM) evaluation increasingly relies on standardized benchmarks to compare models and track progress, yet mounting evidence reveals that these benchmarks are systematically compromised by training data contamination \citep{chang2025benchmarking}. \citet{yang2026rl} demonstrate that rephrased benchmark items appearing in training corpora inflate MMLU accuracy by 8--15 percentage points while evading standard $n$-gram decontamination, and that reinforcement learning (RL) post-training with GRPO further amplifies contamination-driven gains while simultaneously concealing the detection signals that would reveal them. Dose-response analyses confirm a monotonic relationship between contamination exposure and score inflation, with effects persisting across model scales \citep{dong2026dose}. Even minor perturbations to benchmark item surface forms---which should not affect genuinely capable models---produce substantial accuracy drops, questioning the validity of reported scores \citep{wang2025fragility}. Together, these findings point to a reliability crisis in LLM evaluation: reported benchmark scores increasingly conflate memorization with generalizable capability \citep{rao2026silicon}.
5	
6	Several methods have been developed to detect benchmark contamination. Min-K\%++ \citep{duan2025minkpp} and Time Travel \citep{oren2024proving} perform membership inference against pre-training corpora; ConStat \citep{dekoninck2024constat} measures performance gaps between original and paraphrased items; Self-Critique \citep{wust2025selfcritique} identifies memorization through entropy collapse in model self-evaluations; and KDS \citep{xu2025kds} uses knowledge distillation signatures to flag leaked items. While these methods can detect contamination, none produces calibrated benchmark scores that answer the central evaluation question: \emph{what would this model's accuracy be had it never seen the test data?} On the psychometric side, item response theory (IRT) has been increasingly applied to LLM evaluation. PSN-IRT \citep{gu2026psnirt} models item difficulty and discrimination but does not account for contamination. \citet{polo2026growing} validate IRT at the scale of 400+ models yet treat all items as equally trustworthy. ATLAS \citep{lyu2025atlas} and \citet{lalor2024irt} further develop IRT-based evaluation without contamination modeling, and TinyBenchmarks \citep{polo2024tinybenchmarks} demonstrates that carefully selected item subsets can approximate full benchmark scores, but again without contamination awareness. HiBayES \citep{zhuang2025hibayes} provides hierarchical Bayesian uncertainty quantification but ignores item compromise. Inference-time decontamination \citep{yang2024inference} and targeted probing \citep{han2026probing} attempt to adjust or detect contamination effects at evaluation time, but neither produces fully calibrated scores with uncertainty quantification. The closest predecessor, DCR \citep{li2025dcr}, provides adjusted accuracy through fuzzy inference over detection signals but offers no uncertainty bounds and does not address the systematic signal degradation under RL post-training that we document in this work.
7	
8	The case for post-hoc calibration rests on a sobering empirical finding: contamination prevention does not work. \citet{xu2025emperor} systematically evaluate twenty mitigation strategies---including item rephrasing, option shuffling, filtering, and dynamic benchmark construction---and find that none reliably eliminates contamination effects across models and benchmarks. \citet{zhang2025forget} further demonstrate that standard decontamination procedures fail to fully remove memorized content from model weights. While dynamic benchmarks such as LiveBench \citep{white2024livebench} sidestep contamination by continuously generating new items, and efficient evaluation via item subsampling \citep{polo2024tinybenchmarks} can mitigate item-specific biases, neither approach provides calibrated scores for existing contaminated benchmarks. These findings motivate a paradigm shift from contamination \emph{elimination} to contamination \emph{adjustment}: rather than attempting to remove a confounder that resists removal, we measure it and statistically control for it. The analogy to observational causal inference is instructive---when randomization is infeasible, valid estimates are obtained not by eliminating confounders but by measuring and adjusting for them under clearly stated assumptions. ContamCal instantiates this ``statistical safety net'' philosophy for benchmark evaluation.
9	
10	Our approach is grounded in educational measurement's longstanding treatment of item compromise---the leaking of test content to examinees prior to administration. Item compromise IRT models \citep{eckerly2017,sinharay2017} incorporate exposure indicators as covariates within the item response function, enabling joint estimation of compromise effects alongside standard item and person parameters. Explanatory IRT \citep{deboeck2004,adams1997} provides the broader theoretical framework for embedding external covariates in item response models. Adapting this psychometric tradition to LLM evaluation requires three non-trivial structural modifications. First, whereas educational IRT assumes independent examinees, LLM evaluation involves individual models producing all responses to a shared item pool, fundamentally altering local independence semantics \citep{mislevy1986}. Second, binary leakage indicators (compromised vs.\ uncompromised) must be replaced with continuous exposure scores derived from noisy, imperfect detection signals---necessitating a measurement error model that propagates detection uncertainty into ability estimates. Third, stable examinee ability must give way to regime-conditional ability, as models undergoing supervised fine-tuning (SFT) versus RL post-training exhibit qualitatively different contamination signatures that affect both detection signal reliability and contamination effect magnitude.
```

Min-K\\%++ \\citep{duan2025minkpp} and Time Travel \\citep{oren2024proving} perform membership inference against pre-training corpora; ConStat \\citep{dekoninck2024constat} measures performance gaps between original and paraphrased items; Self-Critique \\citep{wust2025selfcritique} identifies memorization through entropy collapse in model self-evaluations; and KDS \\citep{xu2025kds} uses knowledge distillation signatures to flag leaked items. While these methods can detect contamination, none produces calibrated benchmark scores that answer the central evaluation question: \\emph{what would this model's accuracy be had it never seen the test data?} On the psychometric side, item response theory (IRT) has been increasingly applied to LLM evaluation. PSN-IRT \\citep{gu2026psnirt} models item difficulty and discrimination but does not account for contamination. \\citet{polo2026growing} validate IRT at the scale of 400+ models yet treat all items as equally trustworthy. ATLAS \\citep{lyu2025atlas} and \\citet{lalor2024irt} further develop IRT-based evaluation without contamination modeling, and TinyBenchmarks \\citep{polo2024tinybenchmarks} demonstrates that carefully selected item subsets can approximate full benchmark scores, but again without contamination awareness. HiBayES \\citep{zhuang2025hibayes} provides hierarchical Bayesian uncertainty quantification but ignores item compromise. Inference-time decontamination \\citep{yang2024inference} and targeted probing \\citep{han2026probing} attempt to adjust or detect contamination effects at evaluation time, but neither produces fully calibrated scores with uncertainty quantification. The closest predecessor, DCR \\citep{li2025dcr}, provides adjusted accuracy through fuzzy inference over detection signals but offers no uncertainty bounds and does not address the systematic signal degradation under RL post-training that we document in this work.\n\nThe case for post-hoc calibration rests on a sobering empirical finding: contamination prevention does not work. \\citet{xu2025emperor} systematically evaluate twenty mitigation strategies---including item rephrasing, option shuffling, filtering, and dynamic benchmark construction---and find that none reliably eliminates contamination effects across models and benchmarks. \\citet{zhang2025forget} further demonstrate that standard decontamination procedures fail to fully remove memorized content from model weights. While dynamic benchmarks such as LiveBench \\citep{white2024livebench} sidestep contamination by continuously generating new items, and efficient evaluation via item subsampling \\citep{polo2024tinybenchmarks} can mitigate item-specific biases, neither approach provides calibrated scores for existing contaminated benchmarks. These findings motivate a paradigm shift from contamination \\emph{elimination} to contamination \\emph{adjustment}: rather than attempting to remove a confounder that resists removal, we measure it and statistically control for it. The analogy to observational causal inference is instructive---when randomization is infeasible, valid estimates are obtained not by eliminating confounders but by measuring and adjusting for them under clearly stated assumptions. ContamCal instantiates this ``statistical safety net'' philosophy for benchmark evaluation.\n\nOur approach is grounded in educational measurement's longstanding treatment of item compromise---the leaking of test content to examinees prior to administration. Item compromise IRT models \\citep{eckerly2017,sinharay2017} incorporate exposure indicators as covariates within the item response function, enabling joint estimation of compromise effects alongside standard item and person parameters. Explanatory IRT \\citep{deboeck2004,adams1997} provides the broader theoretical framework for embedding external covariates in item response models. Adapting this psychometric tradition to LLM evaluation requires three non-trivial structural modifications. First, whereas educational IRT assumes independent examinees, LLM evaluation involves individual models producing all responses to a shared item pool, fundamentally altering local independence semantics \\citep{mislevy1986}. Second, binary leakage indicators (compromised vs.\\ uncompromised) must be replaced with continuous exposure scores derived from noisy, imperfect detection signals---necessitating a measurement error model that propagates detection uncertainty into ability estimates. Third, stable examinee ability must give way to regime-conditional ability, as models undergoing supervised fine-tuning (SFT) versus RL post-training exhibit qualitatively different contamination signatures that affect both detection signal reliability and contamination effect magnitude.",
      "numLines": 10,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 15
    }
  }
}

{

  "content": "\\section{Introduction}\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nBenchmark scores are the primary currency for comparing language models, yet training data contamination undermines the validity of these comparisons \\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu,chang2025benchmarking}. The problem is not new, but its characterization remains superficial: existing work treats contamination as a binary property of models or benchmarks and proposes aggregate corrections that assume uniform effects across all test items. This framing masks critical structure. Within a single benchmark like MMLU, contamination may strongly inflate scores on mathematics items while leaving humanities items unaffected or producing negative effects. Aggregate corrections that average over such heterogeneity will systematically underestimate the problem in affected domains and overestimate it elsewhere, providing neither accurate calibration nor actionable diagnostic information. Understanding contamination at domain granularity is therefore not merely desirable but necessary for any principled response to the problem.\n\nTwo developments make this gap urgent. First, \\citet{xu2025emperor} systematically evaluate twenty contamination mitigation strategies (rephrasing, option shuffling, dynamic benchmark regeneration, and others) and demonstrate that none restores clean performance after contamination has occurred. This result forecloses the ``prevent contamination'' approach for models already trained on unrestricted web data. Second, the proliferation of open-weight models trained on trillion-token internet corpora means that contamination is now pervasive and cannot be precisely measured at the instance level \\citep{oren2024proving}. Contamination-free benchmarks \\citep{white2024livebench} offer a partial solution but sacrifice the comparability and institutional knowledge accumulated around established benchmarks. The field needs post-hoc statistical tools that quantify contamination effects despite imperfect detection, and that provide domain-level diagnosis rather than aggregate point corrections.\n\nDetection methods such as Min-K\\%++ \\citep{zhang_2024_minkpp}, ConStat \\citep{dekoninck_2024_constat}, and Kernel Divergence Signatures \\citep{choi_2025_kds} can flag contaminated items but stop at detection. They do not quantify how much scores are inflated, in which domains the inflation occurs, or whether effects are uniform across test items. Score adjustment methods like DCR \\citep{li_2025_dcr} provide point corrections without uncertainty quantification or domain-level granularity. IRT-based evaluation frameworks \\citep{polo_2024_tinybenchmarks,habba_2025_growing_pains} use psychometric ability estimation to improve evaluation efficiency and handle new models, but they ignore contamination entirely \\citep{lalor_2024_irt_nlp}. No existing method provides domain-level contamination \\textit{diagnosis} with calibrated uncertainty. This gap prevents practitioners from understanding \\textit{where} their benchmark scores are distorted, \\textit{by how much}, and whether the distortion is uniform or heterogeneous across cognitive domains within a single benchmark.\n\nWe propose ContamCal, a two-stage diagnostic framework that bridges contamination detection and IRT-based evaluation. Stage~1 fuses multiple detection signals into continuous per-item exposure scores via training-free Reciprocal Rank Fusion \\citep{cormack_2009_rrf}, avoiding the meta-circularity of learned aggregators that would require the very contamination labels we lack. Stage~2 embeds these exposure scores as covariates in a contamination-augmented IRT model (2PL+$\\gamma$) with per-domain contamination parameters that reveal where scores are inflated or deflated. The key empirical insight is a phenomenon we term \\textit{dilution masking}: within MMLU, Math and Science domains show strong inflation ($\\gamma \\approx +0.97$ and $+0.84$ on the logit scale) while the pooled estimate across all eight domains is non-significant, because opposite-sign effects in non-STEM domains cancel the STEM inflation at the aggregate level. This cancellation mechanism is structurally analogous to DIF cancellation in educational measurement \\citep{ercikan_2008_dif_cancellation} and renders any aggregate-level contamination analysis fundamentally unreliable when domain effects are heterogeneous.\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Domain-differential contamination characterization.} We demonstrate that MMLU contamination effects are heterogeneous across cognitive domains, with strong STEM inflation ($\\gamma = +0.97$ for Math, $+0.84$ for Science) masked by null or negative non-STEM effects at the aggregate level (dilution masking).\n    \\item \\textbf{Detection signal failure modes.} We document a directional reversal in Min-K\\%++ at model scales below 1.5B parameters (Cohen's $d = -1.55$ to $-1.98$), where the signal is actively misleading rather than merely ineffective.\n    \\item \\textbf{Identifiability boundaries.} We derive Fisher Information bounds showing that reliable $\\gamma$ recovery requires signal AUC $\\geq 0.55$ and ensemble size $J \\geq 50$, revealing a structural mismatch between current LLM evaluation practice ($J = 8$--$30$) and the statistical requirements of contamination-augmented IRT.\n    \\item \\textbf{Conditional calibration with value decomposition.} IRT ability estimation provides calibration benefit independent of $\\gamma$; the contamination parameter serves a complementary diagnostic role that reveals which domains are distorted and by how much, rather than improving overall score accuracy.\n\\end{enumerate}\n"
}

          " ",
          "-We propose ContamCal, a two-stage framework that provides both calibrated benchmark scores and domain-level contamination diagnosis. In Stage~1 (\\emph{Multi-Signal Provenance Mapping}), four training-free detection signals---embedding-based near-duplicate retrieval, Min-K\\%++ membership inference, ConStat performance gaps, and Self-Critique entropy collapse---are aggregated via reciprocal rank fusion \\citep[RRF;][]{cormack2009} to produce per-item exposure scores. RRF is a deliberate design choice: a learned aggregator would face meta-circularity, as its own training data may be contaminated, precluding unbiased weight estimation. In Stage~2 (\\emph{Contamination-Augmented IRT}), exposure scores enter an IRT model as covariates with per-domain contamination effect parameters~$\\gamma_k$, estimated jointly with item and ability parameters via Bayesian inference. A measurement error model treats the observed exposure scores as noisy proxies for latent contamination status, automatically propagating detection uncertainty into posterior estimates. The framework delivers two distinct outputs: (a)~calibrated scores from the IRT ability estimates~$\\theta_i$, obtained by marginalizing over posterior uncertainty with 50\\%/80\\%/95\\% credible intervals; and (b)~domain-level contamination diagnosis from the~$\\gamma_k$ parameters, revealing which cognitive domains are inflated, deflated, or unaffected. Empirically, we find that the calibration benefit arises from IRT ability estimation itself rather than from the explicit $\\gamma$ correction, while $\\gamma$ provides diagnostic information that aggregate metrics cannot capture. Models trained via RL receive appropriately wider intervals, reflecting the genuine epistemic uncertainty that arises when detection signals are degraded.",
