    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:intro}\n\nLarge language model (LLM) evaluation increasingly relies on standardized benchmarks to compare models and track progress, yet mounting evidence reveals that these benchmarks are systematically compromised by training data contamination \\citep{chang2025benchmarking}. \\citet{yang2026rl} demonstrate that rephrased benchmark items appearing in training corpora inflate MMLU accuracy by 8--15 percentage points while evading standard $n$-gram decontamination, and that reinforcement learning (RL) post-training with GRPO further amplifies contamination-driven gains while simultaneously concealing the detection signals that would reveal them. Dose-response analyses confirm a monotonic relationship between contamination exposure and score inflation, with effects persisting across model scales \\citep{dong2026dose}. Even minor perturbations to benchmark item surface forms---which should not affect genuinely capable models---produce substantial accuracy drops, questioning the validity of reported scores \\citep{wang2025fragility}. Together, these findings point to a reliability crisis in LLM evaluation: reported benchmark scores increasingly conflate memorization with generalizable capability \\citep{rao2026silicon}.\n\nSeveral methods have been developed to detect benchmark contamination. Min-K\\%++ \\citep{duan2025minkpp} and Time Travel \\citep{oren2024proving} perform membership inference against pre-training corpora; ConStat \\citep{dekoninck2024constat} measures performance gaps between original and paraphrased items; Self-Critique \\citep{wust2025selfcritique} identifies memorization through entropy collapse in model self-evaluations; and KDS \\citep{xu2025kds} uses knowledge distillation signatures to flag leaked items. While these methods can detect contamination, none produces calibrated benchmark scores that answer the central evaluation question: \\emph{what would this model's accuracy be had it never seen the test data?} On the psychometric side, item response theory (IRT) has been increasingly applied to LLM evaluation. PSN-IRT \\citep{gu2026psnirt} models item difficulty and discrimination but does not account for contamination. \\citet{polo2026growing} validate IRT at the scale of 400+ models yet treat all items as equally trustworthy. ATLAS \\citep{lyu2025atlas} and \\citet{lalor2024irt} further develop IRT-based evaluation without contamination modeling, and TinyBenchmarks \\citep{polo2024tinybenchmarks} demonstrates that carefully selected item subsets can approximate full benchmark scores, but again without contamination awareness. HiBayES \\citep{zhuang2025hibayes} provides hierarchical Bayesian uncertainty quantification but ignores item compromise. Inference-time decontamination \\citep{yang2024inference} and targeted probing \\citep{han2026probing} attempt to adjust or detect contamination effects at evaluation time, but neither produces fully calibrated scores with uncertainty quantification. The closest predecessor, DCR \\citep{li2025dcr}, provides adjusted accuracy through fuzzy inference over detection signals but offers no uncertainty bounds and does not address the systematic signal degradation under RL post-training that we document in this work.\n\nThe case for post-hoc calibration rests on a sobering empirical finding: contamination prevention does not work. \\citet{xu2025emperor} systematically evaluate twenty mitigation strategies---including item rephrasing, option shuffling, filtering, and dynamic benchmark construction---and find that none reliably eliminates contamination effects across models and benchmarks. \\citet{zhang2025forget} further demonstrate that standard decontamination procedures fail to fully remove memorized content from model weights. While dynamic benchmarks such as LiveBench \\citep{white2024livebench} sidestep contamination by continuously generating new items, and efficient evaluation via item subsampling \\citep{polo2024tinybenchmarks} can mitigate item-specific biases, neither approach provides calibrated scores for existing contaminated benchmarks. These findings motivate a paradigm shift from contamination \\emph{elimination} to contamination \\emph{adjustment}: rather than attempting to remove a confounder that resists removal, we measure it and statistically control for it. The analogy to observational causal inference is instructive---when randomization is infeasible, valid estimates are obtained not by eliminating confounders but by measuring and adjusting for them under clearly stated assumptions. ContamCal instantiates this ``statistical safety net'' philosophy for benchmark evaluation.\n\nOur approach is grounded in educational measurement's longstanding treatment of item compromise---the leaking of test content to examinees prior to administration. Item compromise IRT models \\citep{eckerly2017,sinharay2017} incorporate exposure indicators as covariates within the item response function, enabling joint estimation of compromise effects alongside standard item and person parameters. Explanatory IRT \\citep{deboeck2004,adams1997} provides the broader theoretical framework for embedding external covariates in item response models. Adapting this psychometric tradition to LLM evaluation requires three non-trivial structural modifications. First, whereas educational IRT assumes independent examinees, LLM evaluation involves individual models producing all responses to a shared item pool, fundamentally altering local independence semantics \\citep{mislevy1986}. Second, binary leakage indicators (compromised vs.\\ uncompromised) must be replaced with continuous exposure scores derived from noisy, imperfect detection signals---necessitating a measurement error model that propagates detection uncertainty into ability estimates. Third, stable examinee ability must give way to regime-conditional ability, as models undergoing supervised fine-tuning (SFT) versus RL post-training exhibit qualitatively different contamination signatures that affect both detection signal reliability and contamination effect magnitude.\n\nWe propose ContamCal, a two-stage framework that provides both calibrated benchmark scores and domain-level contamination diagnosis. In Stage~1 (\\emph{Multi-Signal Provenance Mapping}), four training-free detection signals---embedding-based near-duplicate retrieval, Min-K\\%++ membership inference, ConStat performance gaps, and Self-Critique entropy collapse---are aggregated via reciprocal rank fusion \\citep[RRF;][]{cormack2009} to produce per-item exposure scores. RRF is a deliberate design choice: a learned aggregator would face meta-circularity, as its own training data may be contaminated, precluding unbiased weight estimation. In Stage~2 (\\emph{Contamination-Augmented IRT}), exposure scores enter an IRT model as covariates with per-domain contamination effect parameters~$\\gamma_k$, estimated jointly with item and ability parameters via Bayesian inference. A measurement error model treats the observed exposure scores as noisy proxies for latent contamination status, automatically propagating detection uncertainty into posterior estimates. The framework delivers two distinct outputs: (a)~calibrated scores from the IRT ability estimates~$\\theta_i$, obtained by marginalizing over posterior uncertainty with 50\\%/80\\%/95\\% credible intervals; and (b)~domain-level contamination diagnosis from the~$\\gamma_k$ parameters, revealing which cognitive domains are inflated, deflated, or unaffected. Empirically, we find that the calibration benefit arises from IRT ability estimation itself rather than from the explicit $\\gamma$ correction, while $\\gamma$ provides diagnostic information that aggregate metrics cannot capture. Models trained via RL receive appropriately wider intervals, reflecting the genuine epistemic uncertainty that arises when detection signals are degraded.\n\nOur contributions are organized hierarchically. At the core: (1)~domain-differential contamination characterization revealing that aggregate benchmark metrics mask heterogeneous effects across cognitive domains (MMLU Math $\\gamma\\!=\\!0.97$ vs.\\ pooled $\\gamma\\!\\approx\\!0$), a dilution masking phenomenon where opposing domain-level effects cancel at the benchmark level; (2)~a signal failure analysis demonstrating that Min-K\\%++ exhibits directional reversal under contamination (Cohen's $d\\!=\\!{-}1.55$ to ${-}1.98$), with consistent reversal direction observed on Llama-3.2-3B (exploratory, $N\\!=\\!4$); and (3)~identifiability boundaries establishing that reliable $\\gamma$ recovery requires $N\\!\\geq\\!50$ models and signal $\\text{AUC}\\!\\geq\\!0.55$, while typical LLM evaluation operates at $N\\!=\\!8$--$30$, creating a domain-method mismatch. Supporting these diagnostic findings: (4)~a calibration value decomposition showing that IRT ability estimation provides calibration (vanilla 2PL matches 2PL+$\\gamma$ on MAE), while the $\\gamma$ parameter independently provides domain-level diagnosis that aggregate methods cannot deliver; and (5)~controlled validation through SFT contamination injection across three model scales (0.5B--3B) within the Qwen2.5 family, with cross-family analysis on Llama-3.2-3B revealing family-specific contamination patterns that support per-family modeling.\n",
    "userModified": false
  }
}
```

```text
5:Two developments make this gap urgent. First, \citet{xu2025emperor} systematically evaluate twenty contamination mitigation strategies (rephrasing, option shuffling, dynamic benchmark regeneration, and others) and demonstrate that none restores clean performance after contamination has occurred. This result forecloses the ``prevent contamination'' approach for models already trained on unrestricted web data. Second, the proliferation of open-weight models trained on trillion-token internet corpora means that contamination is now pervasive and cannot be precisely measured at the instance level \citep{oren2024proving}. Contamination-free benchmarks \citep{white2024livebench} offer a partial solution but sacrifice the comparability and institutional knowledge accumulated around established benchmarks. The field needs post-hoc statistical tools that quantify contamination effects despite imperfect detection, and that provide domain-level diagnosis rather than aggregate point corrections.
```

```text
3:Benchmark scores are the primary currency for comparing language models, yet training data contamination undermines the validity of these comparisons \citep{hendrycks2021mmlu,chang2025benchmarking}. The problem is not new, but its characterization remains superficial: existing work treats contamination as a binary property of models or benchmarks and proposes aggregate corrections that assume uniform effects across all test items. This framing masks critical structure. Within a single benchmark like MMLU, contamination may strongly inflate scores on mathematics items while leaving humanities items unaffected or producing negative effects. Aggregate corrections that average over such heterogeneity will systematically underestimate the problem in affected domains and overestimate it elsewhere, providing neither accurate calibration nor actionable diagnostic information. Understanding contamination at domain granularity is therefore not merely desirable but necessary for any principled response to the problem.
7:Detection methods such as Min-K\%++ \citep{zhang_2024_minkpp}, ConStat \citep{dekoninck_2024_constat}, and Kernel Divergence Signatures \citep{choi_2025_kds} can flag contaminated items but stop at detection. They do not quantify how much scores are inflated, in which domains the inflation occurs, or whether effects are uniform across test items. Score adjustment methods like DCR \citep{li_2025_dcr} provide point corrections without uncertainty quantification or domain-level granularity. IRT-based evaluation frameworks \citep{polo_2024_tinybenchmarks,habba_2025_growing_pains} use psychometric ability estimation to improve evaluation efficiency and handle new models, but they ignore contamination entirely \citep{lalor_2024_irt_nlp}. No existing method provides domain-level contamination \textit{diagnosis} with calibrated uncertainty. This gap prevents practitioners from understanding \textit{where} their benchmark scores are distorted, \textit{by how much}, and whether the distortion is uniform or heterogeneous across cognitive domains within a single benchmark.
9:We propose ContamCal, a two-stage diagnostic framework that bridges contamination detection and IRT-based evaluation. Stage~1 fuses multiple detection signals into continuous per-item exposure scores via training-free Reciprocal Rank Fusion \citep{cormack_2009_rrf}, avoiding the meta-circularity of learned aggregators that would require the very contamination labels we lack. Stage~2 embeds these exposure scores as covariates in a contamination-augmented IRT model (2PL+$\gamma$) with per-domain contamination parameters that reveal where scores are inflated or deflated. The key empirical insight is a phenomenon we term \textit{dilution masking}: within MMLU, Math and Science domains show strong inflation ($\gamma \approx +0.97$ and $+0.84$ on the logit scale) while the pooled estimate across all eight domains is non-significant, because opposite-sign effects in non-STEM domains cancel the STEM inflation at the aggregate level. This cancellation mechanism is structurally analogous to DIF cancellation in educational measurement \citep{ercikan_2008_dif_cancellation} and renders any aggregate-level contamination analysis fundamentally unreliable when domain effects are heterogeneous.
14:    \item \textbf{Detection signal failure modes.} We document a directional reversal in Min-K\%++ at model scales below 1.5B parameters (Cohen's $d = -1.55$ to $-1.98$), where the signal is actively misleading rather than merely ineffective.
15:    \item \textbf{Identifiability boundaries.} We derive Fisher Information bounds showing that reliable $\gamma$ recovery requires signal AUC $\geq 0.55$ and ensemble size $J \geq 50$, revealing a structural mismatch between current LLM evaluation practice ($J = 8$--$30$) and the statistical requirements of contamination-augmented IRT.
16:    \item \textbf{Conditional calibration with value decomposition.} IRT ability estimation provides calibration benefit independent of $\gamma$; the contamination parameter serves a complementary diagnostic role that reveals which domains are distorted and by how much, rather than improving overall score accuracy.
```

{
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_13/projects/contamcal-irt-calibration/artifacts/paper_draft/introduction.tex",
  "content": "\\section{Introduction}\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nBenchmark scores are the primary currency for comparing language models, yet training data contamination undermines the validity of these comparisons \\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu,chang2025benchmarking}. The problem is not new, but its characterization remains superficial: existing work treats contamination as a binary property of models or benchmarks and proposes aggregate corrections that assume uniform effects across all test items. This framing masks critical structure. Within a single benchmark like MMLU, contamination may strongly inflate scores on mathematics items while leaving humanities items unaffected or producing negative effects. Aggregate corrections that average over such heterogeneity will systematically underestimate the problem in affected domains and overestimate it elsewhere. The result is neither accurate calibration nor actionable diagnostic information. Understanding contamination at domain granularity is therefore not merely desirable but necessary for any principled response to the problem.\n\nTwo developments make this gap urgent. First, \\citet{xu2025emperor} systematically evaluate twenty contamination mitigation strategies (rephrasing, option shuffling, dynamic benchmark regeneration, and others) and demonstrate that none restores clean performance after contamination has occurred. This result forecloses the ``prevent contamination'' approach for models already trained on unrestricted web data. Second, the proliferation of open-weight models trained on trillion-token internet corpora means that contamination is now pervasive and cannot be precisely measured at the instance level \\citep{oren2024proving}. Contamination-free benchmarks \\citep{white2024livebench} offer a partial solution but sacrifice the comparability and institutional knowledge accumulated around established benchmarks. Dynamic regeneration of test items may also be contaminated within weeks as web crawlers index benchmark repositories \\citep{dong_2024_cdd}. The field therefore needs post-hoc statistical tools that quantify contamination effects despite imperfect detection, and that provide domain-level diagnosis rather than aggregate point corrections.\n\nDetection methods such as Min-K\\%++ \\citep{zhang_2024_minkpp}, ConStat \\citep{dekoninck_2024_constat}, and Kernel Divergence Signatures \\citep{choi_2025_kds} can flag contaminated items but stop at detection. They do not quantify how much scores are inflated, in which domains the inflation occurs, or whether effects are uniform across test items. Score adjustment methods like DCR \\citep{li_2025_dcr} provide point corrections without uncertainty quantification or domain-level granularity. IRT-based evaluation frameworks \\citep{polo_2024_tinybenchmarks,habba_2025_growing_pains} apply psychometric ability estimation to improve evaluation efficiency and handle new models, but they ignore contamination entirely \\citep{lalor_2024_irt_nlp}. These two research threads have developed in isolation: detection papers end at binary flags, while IRT papers assume all items are equally trustworthy. No existing method provides domain-level contamination \\textit{diagnosis} with calibrated uncertainty. This gap prevents practitioners from understanding \\textit{where} their benchmark scores are distorted, \\textit{by how much}, and whether the distortion is uniform or heterogeneous across cognitive domains within a single benchmark.\n\nWe propose ContamCal, a two-stage diagnostic framework that bridges contamination detection and IRT-based evaluation. Stage~1 fuses multiple detection signals into continuous per-item exposure scores via training-free Reciprocal Rank Fusion \\citep{cormack_2009_rrf}. This design avoids the meta-circularity of learned aggregators that would require the very contamination labels we lack. Stage~2 embeds these exposure scores as covariates in a contamination-augmented IRT model (2PL+$\\gamma$) with per-domain contamination parameters that reveal where scores are inflated or deflated. The key empirical insight is a phenomenon we term \\textit{dilution masking}: within MMLU, Math and Science domains show strong inflation ($\\gamma \\approx +0.97$ and $+0.84$ on the logit scale) while the pooled estimate across all eight domains is non-significant. The mechanism is straightforward: opposite-sign effects in non-STEM domains cancel the STEM inflation at the aggregate level. This cancellation is structurally analogous to DIF cancellation in educational measurement \\citep{ercikan_2008_dif_cancellation}, and it renders any aggregate-level contamination analysis fundamentally unreliable when domain effects are heterogeneous.\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Domain-differential contamination characterization.} We demonstrate that MMLU contamination effects are heterogeneous across cognitive domains, with strong STEM inflation ($\\gamma = +0.97$ for Math, $+0.84$ for Science) masked by null or negative non-STEM effects at the aggregate level. We term this phenomenon dilution masking and show it is a structural property of heterogeneous contamination, not a statistical artifact.\n    \\item \\textbf{Detection signal failure modes.} We document a directional reversal in Min-K\\%++ at model scales below 1.5B parameters (Cohen's $d = -1.55$ to $-1.98$), where the signal is actively misleading rather than merely ineffective. This finding has immediate practical consequences: practitioners applying Min-K\\%++ to small models will reach the opposite of the correct conclusion.\n    \\item \\textbf{Identifiability boundaries.} We derive Fisher Information bounds showing that reliable $\\gamma$ recovery requires signal AUC $\\geq 0.55$ and ensemble size $J \\geq 50$. These thresholds reveal a structural mismatch between current LLM evaluation practice ($J = 8$--$30$) and the statistical requirements of contamination-augmented IRT.\n    \\item \\textbf{Conditional calibration with value decomposition.} IRT ability estimation provides calibration benefit independent of $\\gamma$; the contamination parameter serves a complementary diagnostic role that reveals which domains are distorted and by how much, rather than improving overall score accuracy. This decomposition clarifies the distinct purposes of calibration (how much to trust the score) and diagnosis (where and why the score is distorted).\n\\end{enumerate}\n"
}

The mechanism is straightforward: opposite-sign effects in non-STEM domains cancel the STEM inflation at the aggregate level. This cancellation is structurally analogous to DIF cancellation in educational measurement \\citep{ercikan_2008_dif_cancellation}, and it renders any aggregate-level contamination analysis fundamentally unreliable when domain effects are heterogeneous.",
          " ",
          " Our contributions are as follows:",
          " \\begin{enumerate}",
          "-    \\item \\textbf{Domain-differential contamination characterization.} We demonstrate that MMLU contamination effects are heterogeneous across cognitive domains, with strong STEM inflation ($\\gamma = +0.97$ for Math, $+0.84$ for Science) masked by null or negative non-STEM effects at the aggregate level (dilution masking).",
          "-    \\item \\textbf{Detection signal failure modes.} We document a directional reversal in Min-K\\%++ at model scales below 1.5B parameters (Cohen's $d = -1.55$ to $-1.98$), where the signal is actively misleading rather than merely ineffective.",
          "-    \\item \\textbf{Identifiability boundaries.} We derive Fisher Information bounds showing that reliable $\\gamma$ recovery requires signal AUC $\\geq 0.55$ and ensemble size $J \\geq 50$, revealing a structural mismatch between current LLM evaluation practice ($J = 8$--$30$) and the statistical requirements of contamination-augmented IRT.",
          "-    \\item \\textbf{Conditional calibration with value decomposition.} IRT ability estimation provides calibration benefit independent of $\\gamma$; the contamination parameter serves a complementary diagnostic role that reveals which domains are distorted and by how much, rather than improving overall score accuracy.",
          "+    \\item \\textbf{Domain-differential contamination characterization.} We demonstrate that MMLU contamination effects are heterogeneous across cognitive domains, with strong STEM inflation ($\\gamma = +0.97$ for Math, $+0.84$ for Science) masked by null or negative non-STEM effects at the aggregate level. We term this phenomenon dilution masking and show it is a structural property of heterogeneous contamination, not a statistical artifact.",
          "+    \\item \\textbf{Detection signal failure modes.} We document a directional reversal in Min-K\\%++ at model scales below 1.5B parameters (Cohen's $d = -1.55$ to $-1.98$), where the signal is actively misleading rather than merely ineffective. This finding has immediate practical consequences: practitioners applying Min-K\\%++ to small models will reach the opposite of the correct conclusion.",
          "+    \\item \\textbf{Identifiability boundaries.} We derive Fisher Information bounds showing that reliable $\\gamma$ recovery requires signal AUC $\\geq 0.55$ and ensemble size $J \\geq 50$. These thresholds reveal a structural mismatch between current LLM evaluation practice ($J = 8$--$30$) and the statistical requirements of contamination-augmented IRT.",
          "+    \\item \\textbf{Conditional calibration with value decomposition.} IRT ability estimation provides calibration benefit independent of $\\gamma$; the contamination parameter serves a complementary diagnostic role that reveals which domains are distorted and by how much, rather than improving overall score accuracy. This decomposition clarifies the distinct purposes of calibration (how much to trust the score) and diagnosis (where and why the score is distorted).",
          " \\end{enumerate}"
        ]
      }
    ],
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nBenchmark scores are the primary currency for comparing language models, yet training data contamination undermines the validity of these comparisons \\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu,chang2025benchmarking}. The problem is not new, but its characterization remains superficial: existing work treats contamination as a binary property of models or benchmarks and proposes aggregate corrections that assume uniform effects across all test items. This framing masks critical structure. Within a single benchmark like MMLU, contamination may strongly inflate scores on mathematics items while leaving humanities items unaffected or producing negative effects. Aggregate corrections that average over such heterogeneity will systematically underestimate the problem in affected domains and overestimate it elsewhere, providing neither accurate calibration nor actionable diagnostic information. Understanding contamination at domain granularity is therefore not merely desirable but necessary for any principled response to the problem.\n\nTwo developments make this gap urgent. First, \\citet{xu2025emperor} systematically evaluate twenty contamination mitigation strategies (rephrasing, option shuffling, dynamic benchmark regeneration, and others) and demonstrate that none restores clean performance after contamination has occurred. This result forecloses the ``prevent contamination'' approach for models already trained on unrestricted web data. Second, the proliferation of open-weight models trained on trillion-token internet corpora means that contamination is now pervasive and cannot be precisely measured at the instance level \\citep{oren2024proving}. Contamination-free benchmarks \\citep{white2024livebench} offer a partial solution but sacrifice the comparability and institutional knowledge accumulated around established benchmarks. The field needs post-hoc statistical tools that quantify contamination effects despite imperfect detection, and that provide domain-level diagnosis rather than aggregate point corrections.\n\nDetection methods such as Min-K\\%++ \\citep{zhang_2024_minkpp}, ConStat \\citep{dekoninck_2024_constat}, and Kernel Divergence Signatures \\citep{choi_2025_kds} can flag contaminated items but stop at detection. They do not quantify how much scores are inflated, in which domains the inflation occurs, or whether effects are uniform across test items. Score adjustment methods like DCR \\citep{li_2025_dcr} provide point corrections without uncertainty quantification or domain-level granularity. IRT-based evaluation frameworks \\citep{polo_2024_tinybenchmarks,habba_2025_growing_pains} use psychometric ability estimation to improve evaluation efficiency and handle new models, but they ignore contamination entirely \\citep{lalor_2024_irt_nlp}. No existing method provides domain-level contamination \\textit{diagnosis} with calibrated uncertainty. This gap prevents practitioners from understanding \\textit{where} their benchmark scores are distorted, \\textit{by how much}, and whether the distortion is uniform or heterogeneous across cognitive domains within a single benchmark.\n\nWe propose ContamCal, a two-stage diagnostic framework that bridges contamination detection and IRT-based evaluation. Stage~1 fuses multiple detection signals into continuous per-item exposure scores via training-free Reciprocal Rank Fusion \\citep{cormack_2009_rrf}, avoiding the meta-circularity of learned aggregators that would require the very contamination labels we lack. Stage~2 embeds these exposure scores as covariates in a contamination-augmented IRT model (2PL+$\\gamma$) with per-domain contamination parameters that reveal where scores are inflated or deflated. The key empirical insight is a phenomenon we term \\textit{dilution masking}: within MMLU, Math and Science domains show strong inflation ($\\gamma \\approx +0.97$ and $+0.84$ on the logit scale) while the pooled estimate across all eight domains is non-significant, because opposite-sign effects in non-STEM domains cancel the STEM inflation at the aggregate level. This cancellation mechanism is structurally analogous to DIF cancellation in educational measurement \\citep{ercikan_2008_dif_cancellation} and renders any aggregate-level contamination analysis fundamentally unreliable when domain effects are heterogeneous.\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Domain-differential contamination characterization.} We demonstrate that MMLU contamination effects are heterogeneous across cognitive domains, with strong STEM inflation ($\\gamma = +0.97$ for Math, $+0.84$ for Science) masked by null or negative non-STEM effects at the aggregate level (dilution masking).\n    \\item \\textbf{Detection signal failure modes.} We document a directional reversal in Min-K\\%++ at model scales below 1.5B parameters (Cohen's $d = -1.55$ to $-1.98$), where the signal is actively misleading rather than merely ineffective.\n    \\item \\textbf{Identifiability boundaries.} We derive Fisher Information bounds showing that reliable $\\gamma$ recovery requires signal AUC $\\geq 0.55$ and ensemble size $J \\geq 50$, revealing a structural mismatch between current LLM evaluation practice ($J = 8$--$30$) and the statistical requirements of contamination-augmented IRT.\n    \\item \\textbf{Conditional calibration with value decomposition.} IRT ability estimation provides calibration benefit independent of $\\gamma$; the contamination parameter serves a complementary diagnostic role that reveals which domains are distorted and by how much, rather than improving overall score accuracy.\n\\end{enumerate}\n",
    "userModified": false
  }
}

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_011dbZkkLEgazGBwL9yKYKTs`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}
2	
3	Benchmark scores are the primary currency for comparing language models, yet training data contamination undermines the validity of these comparisons \citep{hendrycks2021mmlu,chang2025benchmarking}. The problem is not new, but its characterization remains superficial: existing work treats contamination as a binary property of models or benchmarks and proposes aggregate corrections that assume uniform effects across all test items. This framing masks critical structure. Within a single benchmark like MMLU, contamination may strongly inflate scores on mathematics items while leaving humanities items unaffected or producing negative effects. Aggregate corrections that average over such heterogeneity will systematically underestimate the problem in affected domains and overestimate it elsewhere. The result is neither accurate calibration nor actionable diagnostic information. Understanding contamination at domain granularity is therefore not merely desirable but necessary for any principled response to the problem.
4	
5	Two developments make this gap urgent. First, \citet{xu2025emperor} systematically evaluate twenty contamination mitigation strategies (rephrasing, option shuffling, dynamic benchmark regeneration, and others) and demonstrate that none restores clean performance after contamination has occurred. This result forecloses the ``prevent contamination'' approach for models already trained on unrestricted web data. Second, the proliferation of open-weight models trained on trillion-token internet corpora means that contamination is now pervasive and cannot be precisely measured at the instance level \citep{oren2024proving}. Contamination-free benchmarks \citep{white2024livebench} offer a partial solution but sacrifice the comparability and institutional knowledge accumulated around established benchmarks. Dynamic regeneration of test items may also be contaminated within weeks as web crawlers index benchmark repositories \citep{dong_2024_cdd}. The field therefore needs post-hoc statistical tools that quantify contamination effects despite imperfect detection, and that provide domain-level diagnosis rather than aggregate point corrections.
6	
7	Detection methods such as Min-K\%++ \citep{zhang_2024_minkpp}, ConStat \citep{dekoninck_2024_constat}, and Kernel Divergence Signatures \citep{choi_2025_kds} can flag contaminated items but stop at detection. They do not quantify how much scores are inflated, in which domains the inflation occurs, or whether effects are uniform across test items. Score adjustment methods like DCR \citep{li_2025_dcr} provide point corrections without uncertainty quantification or domain-level granularity. IRT-based evaluation frameworks \citep{polo_2024_tinybenchmarks,habba_2025_growing_pains} apply psychometric ability estimation to improve evaluation efficiency and handle new models, but they ignore contamination entirely \citep{lalor_2024_irt_nlp}. These two research threads have developed in isolation: detection papers end at binary flags, while IRT papers assume all items are equally trustworthy. No existing method provides domain-level contamination \textit{diagnosis} with calibrated uncertainty. This gap prevents practitioners from understanding \textit{where} their benchmark scores are distorted, \textit{by how much}, and whether the distortion is uniform or heterogeneous across cognitive domains within a single benchmark.
8	
9	We propose ContamCal, a two-stage diagnostic framework that bridges contamination detection and IRT-based evaluation. Stage~1 fuses multiple detection signals into continuous per-item exposure scores via training-free Reciprocal Rank Fusion \citep{cormack_2009_rrf}. This design avoids the meta-circularity of learned aggregators that would require the very contamination labels we lack. Stage~2 embeds these exposure scores as covariates in a contamination-augmented IRT model (2PL+$\gamma$) with per-domain contamination parameters that reveal where scores are inflated or deflated. The key empirical insight is a phenomenon we term \textit{dilution masking}: within MMLU, Math and Science domains show strong inflation ($\gamma \approx +0.97$ and $+0.84$ on the logit scale) while the pooled estimate across all eight domains is non-significant. The mechanism is straightforward: opposite-sign effects in non-STEM domains cancel the STEM inflation at the aggregate level. This cancellation is structurally analogous to DIF cancellation in educational measurement \citep{ercikan_2008_dif_cancellation}, and it renders any aggregate-level contamination analysis fundamentally unreliable when domain effects are heterogeneous.
10	
11	Our contributions are as follows:
12	\begin{enumerate}
13	    \item \textbf{Domain-differential contamination characterization.} We demonstrate that MMLU contamination effects are heterogeneous across cognitive domains, with strong STEM inflation ($\gamma = +0.97$ for Math, $+0.84$ for Science) masked by null or negative non-STEM effects at the aggregate level. We term this phenomenon dilution masking and show it is a structural property of heterogeneous contamination, not a statistical artifact.
14	    \item \textbf{Detection signal failure modes.} We document a directional reversal in Min-K\%++ at model scales below 1.5B parameters (Cohen's $d = -1.55$ to $-1.98$), where the signal is actively misleading rather than merely ineffective. This finding has immediate practical consequences: practitioners applying Min-K\%++ to small models will reach the opposite of the correct conclusion.
15	    \item \textbf{Identifiability boundaries.} We derive Fisher Information bounds showing that reliable $\gamma$ recovery requires signal AUC $\geq 0.55$ and ensemble size $J \geq 50$. These thresholds reveal a structural mismatch between current LLM evaluation practice ($J = 8$--$30$) and the statistical requirements of contamination-augmented IRT.
16	    \item \textbf{Conditional calibration with value decomposition.} IRT ability estimation provides calibration benefit independent of $\gamma$; the contamination parameter serves a complementary diagnostic role that reveals which domains are distorted and by how much, rather than improving overall score accuracy. This decomposition clarifies the distinct purposes of calibration (how much to trust the score) and diagnosis (where and why the score is distorted).
17	\end{enumerate}
18	
```
